The precise proposed estimate and its scope\n\nFor a feature map h:R^d -> R^m consider the exact gradient system\n\n    x'=J(x)^T c,        c'=h(x),        J=Dh,\n    A(x,c)=sum_a c_a D^2 h_a(x).\n\nIts tangent generator is the symmetric matrix\n\n    L(t)=[ A(t)  J(t)^T ; J(t)  0 ].                    (1)\n\nFor variation in the initial hidden state with initial readout fixed,\nwrite Y=(P;R), with\n\n    P'=A P+J^T R,       R'=J P,       P(0)=I, R(0)=0.   (2)\n\nThe tested premises on [0,S] are\n\n    ||J(t)||_op <= B,       integral_0^S ||A(t)||_* dt <= L_A.\n\nThey do not imply any finite deterministic upper bound, depending only\non (d,m,S,B,L_A), for\n\n    integral_0^S log det(I_m+K(t)K(t)^T) dt,\n    K=R P^(-1).                                        (3)\n\nThe examples below have d=m=2, S=4, common finite B and L_A, uniformly\nbounded features and primal states, and P invertible at EVERY time.\nThus allowing isolated singularities of P does not repair this estimate.\n\nThey also disprove any uniform decay-to-zero estimate for the time spent\nwith (3)'s integrand above a large threshold. (4)\n\nTo describe the evolving tangent plane through vertical projections,\nchoose an orthonormal frame Q=(E;F), Q^T Q=I_d, with Q(0)=(I_d;0),\ntransported by\n\n    Q'=(I-QQ^T)LQ.\n\nPut M=Q^T LQ. Then\n\n    E'=A E+J^T F-E M,\n    F'=J E-F M,\n    M=E^T A E+E^T J^T F+F^T J E.                       (5)\n\nThese equations preserve Q^T Q=I and span the same plane as Y: if\nT'=M T, T(0)=I, then Y=QT solves Y'=LY. Hence K=F E^(-1) whenever\nthe hidden projection is nonsingular. From\n\n    E^T (I_d+K^T K) E=I_d\n\nand the determinant identity det(I+KK^T)=det(I+K^TK), the angle functional\nis exactly\n\n    Psi=log det(I_m+KK^T)=-2 log|det E|.                (6)\n\nThus, whenever E is invertible,\n\n    Psi'=2[Tr M-Tr A-Tr(J^T K)].                        (7)\n\nThe hypotheses really do control the motion of the plane:\n\n    integral_0^S ||Q'||_* dt <= L_A+2m B S,             (8)\n    |Tr M| <= ||A||_*+2m B.\n\nIndeed ||(I-QQ^T)LQ||_*<=||L||_* and\n||L||_*<=||A||_*+2||J||_*<=||A||_*+2mB. These estimates control how\nfar the plane moves. They do not prohibit approaching a vertical plane\nand then remaining near it. The unbounded logarithmic cost in (6)\ncannot be estimated by the bounded path length in (8).\n\nFor one isolated two-dimensional tangent block, put\ntheta=atan(r/p), continuously on an interval with p>0. Equations (2)\nreduce exactly to\n\n    theta'=j cos(2theta)-(a/2) sin(2theta).              (9)\n\nThe construction below realizes this equation inside a true autonomous\nbounded-feature gradient flow. It does not merely prescribe a symmetric\ntime-dependent matrix.\n\n## 3. A bounded smooth feature map realizing a nearly vertical plateau\n\nUse hidden coordinates (u,v) and readout coordinates (c_1,c_2). Let\n\n    g(v)=arctan v,           b(u)=(1-exp(-u^2))/2,\n    h_1(u,v)=j(v) tanh u,\n    h_2(u,v)=g(v)+a(v)b(u).                              (10)\n\nThe functions j,a will be smooth and compactly supported. Since tanh,\ng,b and their first derivatives are bounded, h and Dh are globally\nbounded. The family of j,a below has common bounds on every fixed-order\nderivative, so these feature and derivative bounds are uniform in the\nparameter of the example.\n\nInitialize the reference trajectory at\n\n    u(0)=0, v(0)=1, c_1(0)=c_2(0)=0.\n\nExactly, for every choice of j,a,\n\n    u=c_1=0,        v'=c_2 g'(v),        c_2'=g(v).      (11)\n\nOn [0,4], v>=1, c_2>=0, c_2<=pi t/2, and\n\n    1<=v(t)<=1+pi t^2/4.\n\nMoreover c_2(t)>0 and v'(t)>0 for t>0, so t can be used as a smooth\nfunction of v away from t=0. This allows compactly supported smooth\nprofiles in t, supported away from zero, to be realized as functions of\nv along this exact trajectory.\n\nChoose nonnegative smooth bumps beta_i of integral one, supported inside\n(i-1,i), for i=1,2,3. For a parameter q>0 prescribe\n\n    j_*(t)=beta_1(t)-q beta_3(t),\n    A_*(t)=-2 beta_2(t).                                (12)\n\nOn their supports set\n\n    j(v(t))=j_*(t),       a(v(t))=A_*(t)/c_2(t),\n\nand extend both by zero outside the corresponding compact v intervals.\nSmoothness follows because all supports avoid t=0 and their endpoints.\nThe resulting feature map (10) is a single autonomous map, not a\ntime-dependent or trajectory-forced vector field.\n\nAlong (11) its exact tangent matrices are diagonal:\n\n    J=diag(j_*,g'(v)),\n    A=diag(A_*,c_2 g''(v)).                              (13)\n\nIn particular the transverse initial-hidden variation (p,r) obeys\n\n    p'=A_*p+j_*r,       r'=j_*p,       p(0)=1,r(0)=0.    (14)\n\nThe three separated phases yield\n\n    p(1)=cosh 1,                 r(1)=sinh 1,\n    p(2)=exp(-2) cosh 1=:p_*,    r(2)=sinh 1=:r_*.\n\nHere 0<p_*<r_*. The last phase has\nintegrated j_* equal to -q, and therefore\n\n    p(3)=p_* cosh q-r_* sinh q=rho_* sinh epsilon,\n    r(3)=r_* cosh q-p_* sinh q=rho_* cosh epsilon.       (15)\n\nFor the entire interval [3,4], j_*=A_*=0. Consequently p and r remain\nconstant there and\n\n    K_(11)(t)=r(t)/p(t)=coth epsilon,        3<=t<=4.    (16)\n\nThere is no earlier zero of p: it is positive in the first two phases,\nand in the third phase it equals\nrho_* sinh(q_*-q_accumulated), where 0<=q_accumulated<=q_* - epsilon.\n\nThe longitudinal hidden derivative p_v is also strictly positive. It\nobeys p_v'=c_2g'' p_v+g' r_v, r_v'=g'p_v, starting from (1,0), and\ng'>0. Before any proposed first zero, r_v>=0; the integrating-factor\nformula for p_v is a positive exponential times 1 plus a nonnegative\nintegral. A first zero is therefore impossible. Since the two blocks\ndecouple on the reference trajectory, P is diagonal and invertible on\nall of [0,4].\n\n## 4. Uniform premises and unbounded logarithmic occupation\n\nAll q in the construction lie in a fixed compact interval. Thus j,a,\ntheir required derivatives, h, and Dh have bounds independent of epsilon.\nThe reference primal states in (11) are independent of epsilon.\n\nThe nuclear Hessian action has the exact finite value\n\n    integral_0^4 ||A(t)||_* dt\n       =2+integral_0^4 |c_2 g''(v)|dt\n       =2+log[(1+v(4)^2)/2].                            (17)\n\nFor the last equality, v'=c_2g'(v) and g''(v)<0 for v>=1 give\nc_2g''(v)=d/dt log g'(v). This bound is independent of epsilon and,\nwith m=2 fixed, is in particular O(m). No cancellation between positive\nand negative eigenvalues is being used.\n\nOn [3,4], (16) and tanh epsilon<=epsilon imply\n\n    Psi(t)>=log(1+coth^2 epsilon)>=2 log(1/epsilon).\n\nTherefore\n\n    integral_0^4 Psi(t)dt >=2 log(1/epsilon) -> infinity. (18)\n\nFor every threshold R, choosing epsilon<=exp(-R/2) gives a full time\ninterval of length one on which Psi>=R. Equivalently one principal angle\nhas cosine at most epsilon on that interval. Thus bounded J and bounded\nnuclear A action give neither the proposed integral bound nor a uniform\ndecaying tail for near-vertical occupation.\n\nThe feature bounds also ensure global existence of these example flows:\n|c_t|<=|c_0|+S sup|h| and |x_t-x_0|<=sup||J|| times the integral of\n|c_t|. Thus this obstruction does not arise from primal blow-up.\n\n## 5. The counterexample can be real analytic\n\nThis refinement prevents interpreting the obstruction as an artifact of\nflat smooth profiles. It is still not the canonical network.\n\nFor each epsilon, convolve the compact smooth functions j,a in (10) with\na centered Gaussian mollifier of sufficiently small variance. Denote the\nresults j_delta,a_delta. They are real analytic, globally bounded, and\ntheir k-th derivatives have sup norm at most that of the original k-th\nderivatives for every fixed k. They converge uniformly with their first\ntwo derivatives to j,a as delta tends to zero.\n\nUse j_delta,a_delta in (10). The resulting feature map is bounded and\nreal analytic on R^2, with the same uniform global feature and Dh bounds.\nThe reference trajectory (11) is EXACTLY unchanged because\ntanh(0)=b(0)=b'(0)=0. Its transverse coefficients converge uniformly on\n[0,4] to those in (14). All transverse coefficient matrices are uniformly\nbounded, so the integral equation for the two-dimensional linear system,\nfollowed by Gronwall, bounds the uniform solution error by a fixed\nconstant times the coefficient error.\n\nChoose delta so this solution error is less than\nrho_* sinh(epsilon)/2. Then p_delta remains positive throughout [0,4].\nOn [3,4] it is at most (3/2)rho_* sinh epsilon, while r_delta is bounded\nbelow by a positive constant independent of small epsilon. Thus\n\n    Psi_delta(t)>=2 log(1/epsilon)-C,       3<=t<=4.\n\nThe nuclear action in (17) changes by at most\n4 sup_[0,4] c_2 times ||a_delta-a||_infinity, so a single enlarged\nconstant bounds it for the entire analytic family. This proves the same\nunbounded integrated logarithmic occupation for analytic examples, with\nno singular hidden Jacobian and no interval of exactly zero transverse\ncoefficients. No width-uniform analytic radius is asserted or needed.\n\n## Outcome and exact limitation\n\nThe specified deterministic angle route is falsified under its stated\npremises. Even bounded analytic features, a common global J bound,\nbounded primal states, and an O(m) nuclear Hessian-action integral allow\narbitrarily large time-integrated logarithmic projection distortion.\n\nThis does not establish a failure of an averaged estimate under the\ncanonical Gaussian initialization. It provides no canonical reachable\ncounterexample, no large-Gaussian-probability statement for the example,\nand no lower bound on canonical projected entropy. It shows precisely\nthat the proposed deterministic estimate cannot be deduced from the\nlisted structural and norm assumptions alone.\n", "truncated": false}}

ASSISTANT: The scalar-readout proof now gives a quantitative bound, not just invertibility. But a separate construction shows that bounded derivatives and bounded volume distortion alone do not control projection loss for vector readouts—even for analytic dynamics. That rules out this particular shortcut; it does not disprove the three-hidden-layer theorem.

TOOL {"type": "commandExecution", "id": "exec-464eb5b4-4047-472e-8094-231d3f3aecd9", "command": "/bin/bash -lc \"sed -n '1,300p' /tmp/l3-full-resolution-9nbD4z/SCALAR_READOUT_PROJECTION_THEOREM.md\"", "cwd": "/home/amir/Codes/PDE", "status": "completed", "exitCode": 0, "durationMs": 0, "output": {"text": "# Scalar-readout hidden projection: a finite-width theorem\n\nStatus: proved for a bounded smooth scalar feature map. The fixed-readout\nhidden projection is a global orientation-preserving diffeomorphism at\nevery finite feature time. For zero initial readout it is also a global\norientation-preserving diffeomorphism at every finite forward physical\ntime. In both cases its log determinant is bounded below by the integrated\ndivergence of the complete state flow. The canonical arctan network at\nwidth n=1 consequently has finite hidden Gaussian relative entropy with\nthe finite-time bounds stated below.\n\nThis is a scalar-readout theorem, not a width-uniform theorem. In the\ncanonical network the readout has dimension n, so the theorem applies\ndirectly only when n=1. Nothing here proves a corresponding assertion for\nn>=2, a global mean-field limit, or physical-time injectivity for an\narbitrary nonzero initial scalar readout. The latter does not follow by\nsubstituting an initial-data-dependent feature clock into the feature-time\ntheorem.\n\n## 1. Setup and completeness of the auxiliary gradient flow\n\nLet h:R^d -> R be smooth and bounded, with |h|<=a<infinity. No global\nbound on its derivatives is assumed. Consider the feature-time system\n\n    x'=c grad h(x),                 c'=h(x),               (1)\n\nwith a fixed deterministic scalar initial readout c_0. Write\n\n    b(x,c)=(c grad h(x),h(x)),\n    A(x,c)=c D^2 h(x),\n    Db=[ A  grad h ; (grad h)^T  0 ].                       (2)\n\nThe hidden initial-data map is F_(s,c_0)(x_0)=x_s(x_0,c_0), and its\nd-by-d derivative is P_s=D_(x_0)F_(s,c_0).\n\nLet psi_r be the flow of grad h. This auxiliary flow is complete in both\ndirections. Indeed, on any existing forward segment of length R,\n\n    integral_0^R |grad h(psi_r x)|^2 dr\n         =h(psi_R x)-h(x) <= 2a.\n\nCauchy--Schwarz therefore gives\n\n    integral_0^R |grad h(psi_r x)| dr <= sqrt(2aR).          (3)\n\nIf a maximal forward interval had a finite endpoint, (3) would keep its\ntrajectory in a bounded set up to that endpoint. Smooth local ODE\nexistence on the closure of that bounded set extends the solution, a\ncontradiction. Apply the same argument to -grad h, using the decrease of\nh, to obtain backward completeness. This proof does not assume bounded\ngrad h.\n\nAlong any local solution of (1), set tau_s=integral_0^s c_u du. Uniqueness\nand the chain rule give\n\n    x_s=psi_(tau_s)(x_0).\n\nMoreover,\n\n    |c_s|<=|c_0|+a|s|,\n    |tau_s|<=|c_0||s|+a s^2/2.                             (4)\n\nThe representation and completeness of psi keep x_s in a compact\ngradient-orbit segment on every bounded feature interval. Together with\nthe readout bound this proves complete existence of (1), and smooth\ndependence on its initial data.\n\nEvery hidden trajectory stays in its initial gradient orbit. A critical\npoint of h is a singleton orbit and remains fixed in the hidden\ncoordinates, even if its readout changes. On a noncritical orbit choose\n\n    gamma(r)=psi_r(x_*),             H(r)=h(gamma(r)).\n\nIts parameter r runs through R, and\n\n    H'(r)=|grad h(gamma(r))|^2>0.                           (5)\n\nIn particular gamma never repeats a point. Distinct gradient orbits are\ndisjoint by uniqueness of the auxiliary flow. No smooth quotient of the\nset of orbits will be needed.\n\n## 2. Feature-time order and exact determinant\n\nOn the orbit gamma, (1) is exactly\n\n    r'=c,                    c'=H(r),\n    r(0)=r_0,                c(0)=c_0.                    (6)\n\nFix c_0 and differentiate in r_0. If p=partial r_s/partial r_0, then\n\n    p''=H'(r_s)p,\n    p(0)=1,                  p'(0)=0.                    (7)\n\nSince H'>=0, p remains positive: while p is positive its second\nderivative is nonnegative, hence p'>=0 and p>=1 for s>=0; a first failure\nof positivity is therefore impossible. Replacing s by -s proves the\nsame assertion backward. Consequently\n\n    p(s)>=1 for every real s.                             (8)\n\nFor every r_0 the displacement satisfies the uniform bound (4).\nTherefore r_0 -> r_s(r_0) is strictly increasing and tends to the\ncorresponding signed infinity as r_0 does. It is onto R. Thus F_(s,c_0)\nis bijective on each noncritical gradient orbit, fixes every critical\npoint, and is globally bijective on R^d.\n\nThe local determinant is also everywhere positive. For a fixed x_0 put\ng_0=grad h(x_0) and G_s=D_x psi_(tau_s)(x_0), where the latter derivative\nholds the flow time tau_s fixed. The flow identity\n\n    G_s g_0=grad h(x_s)\n\nand differentiation of x_s=psi_(tau_s(x_0))(x_0) give\n\n    P_s=G_s [I+g_0 (D_(x_0)tau_s)].                        (9)\n\nThe scalar factor in the determinant lemma is exactly the orbit\nderivative p(s): perturbing x_0 to psi_epsilon(x_0) gives the final orbit\ncoordinate epsilon+tau_s(psi_epsilon(x_0)), whose derivative at zero is\n\n    p(s)=1+(D_(x_0)tau_s)g_0.                             (10)\n\nAt a critical point this factor is 1. Applying the usual determinant\nODE to the complete auxiliary gradient flow and then the chain rule\nalong tau_s yields the exact identity\n\n    det P_s\n       =exp(integral_0^s c_u Delta h(x_u) du) p(s)\n       =exp(integral_0^s Tr A(x_u,c_u) du) p(s).           (11)\n\nThis change of parameter uses an antiderivative along psi and does not\nrequire tau_s to be monotone. Formula (11) is valid at critical points\nwith p=1 as well. Thus P_s is everywhere nonsingular. Global bijectivity\nand the inverse function theorem make F_(s,c_0) a global\norientation-preserving smooth diffeomorphism.\n\nIn particular, for either sign of feature time,\n\n    log det P_s >= integral_0^s Tr A(x_u,c_u) du\n                = integral_0^s div b(x_u,c_u) du.          (12)\n\nThere is also a branchwise response identity. Let R_s=D_(x_0)c_s and\nK_s=R_s P_s^(-1). Orbit differentiation gives\n\n    P_s g_0=p(s) grad h(x_s),       R_s g_0=p'(s).\n\nConsequently\n\n    K_s grad h(x_s)=p'(s)/p(s)>=0,              s>=0.     (13)\n\nAt a critical point both sides are zero. This is the fixed-c_0 graph\nslope. It is not the derivative of a conditional mean obtained by mixing\ndifferent initial readouts.\n\n## 3. Forward physical time at zero initial readout\n\nNow take c_0=0 and use the physical field\n\n    dot x=alpha c grad h(x),       dot c=alpha h(x),\n    f=c h(x),                     alpha=2(1-f).           (14)\n\nHere f=c h is the scalar, n=1 normalization. Along any local forward\nsolution,\n\n    dot f=2(1-f)(h^2+c^2|grad h|^2),       f(0)=0.\n\nThe residual equation implies 0<=f<1 on each finite existing interval,\nand therefore 0<alpha<=2. The feature-clock length s(t)=integral_0^t\nalpha_u du is at most 2t. Equations (4) and the complete feature flow\nthen prove forward physical completeness and the bounds\n\n    |c_t|<=2at,\n    |tau_t|<=2at^2,            tau_t=integral_0^t alpha_u c_u du.  (15)\n\nThe hidden state still has the representation x_t=psi_(tau_t)(x_0).\nThe following proof concerns the fixed physical time t directly; it does\nnot infer injectivity from the feature-time theorem at a varying clock.\n\nOn a noncritical gradient orbit, (14) becomes\n\n    dot r=2(1-cH)c,            dot c=2(1-cH)H.\n\nSet p=partial r_t/partial r_0 and q=partial c_t/partial r_0, keeping\nc_0=0 fixed. Direct differentiation gives\n\n    dot p=-2c^2 H' p+2(1-2f)q,\n    dot q=2(1-2f)H' p-2H^2 q,\n    p(0)=1,                   q(0)=0.                    (16)\n\nChoose an antiderivative U'=H. The exact invariant\nc^2-2U(r)=-2U(r_0) gives\n\n    c q=H p-H(r_0).                                      (17)\n\nFirst suppose H_0=H(r_0) is nonzero. The equations preserve the sign of\nH_0 in H and in c for t>0: if H_0>0, c increases from zero and H is\nnondecreasing; if H_0<0, c decreases from zero and H is nonincreasing.\nThus H_0/c>0 for t>0. Also f increases strictly from zero while remaining\nless than one.\n\nUntil f reaches 1/2, the off-diagonal coefficients in (16) are\nnonnegative. The integral forms with the diagonal integrating factors\ngive p>0 and q>=0 on that interval. If a first zero of p occurred later,\nthen f>1/2 there and (17) would imply\n\n    dot p=-2(1-2f)H_0/c>0.\n\nA first zero approached from positive values cannot have positive\nderivative. Hence p remains strictly positive at every finite time.\n\nIf instead H_0=0, the state with c_0=0 is stationary. Linearizing (16)\nat that state gives\n\n    p(t)=cosh(2 sqrt(H'(r_0)) t)>0.                       (18)\n\nThere is a quantitative lower bound as well. Before f=1/2,\n\n    dot p>=-2c^2 H' p>=-2(c^2 H'+H^2)p.\n\nAfter f>=1/2, use (17) to rewrite the first equation of (16):\n\n    dot p=[-2c^2 H'+2(1-2f)H/c]p\n                         -2(1-2f)H_0/c.\n\nThe last term is nonnegative. Since 0<f<1,\n\n    H/c=H^2/f>=H^2,\n    2(1-2f)H/c=2H/c-4H^2>=-2H^2.\n\nThus throughout the trajectory\n\n    log p(t)>=-2 integral_0^t (c_u^2 H'(r_u)+H(r_u)^2) du.  (19)\n\nFor H_0=0 this follows directly from (18), since H=c=0 along the\nstationary state. Critical hidden points are singleton orbits and are\ntreated separately: the determinant factor in (10) is exactly p=1,\nso positivity and (19) also hold there, including when h is nonzero.\n\nThe determinant argument (9)--(11), now with dot tau=alpha c, gives\n\n    det P_t=exp(integral_0^t alpha_u c_u Delta h(x_u) du) p(t)>0. (20)\n\nEvery noncritical orbit map has positive derivative and displacement\nbounded uniformly in its initial orbit coordinate by (15). It is\ntherefore increasing and onto R. Critical points are fixed. As before,\nthis proves that the physical hidden map with c_0=0 is a global\norientation-preserving smooth diffeomorphism.\n\nThe complete physical Jacobian has the exact rank-one correction\n\n    D(alpha b)=alpha Db-2 b b^T,\n    div(alpha b)=alpha c Delta h-2(c^2|grad h|^2+h^2).     (21)\n\nCombining (19)--(21) proves\n\n    log det P_t >= integral_0^t div(alpha b)(x_u,c_u) du.  (22)\n\nNo assertion for arbitrary nonzero c_0 in physical time is made here.\nIn particular, neither (16)--(19) nor the required sign H_0/c>0 has been\nestablished for such initial data by this argument.\n\n## 4. The scalar feature is\nbounded by a=pi/2, but its derivatives need not be globally bounded in\nthe hidden parameters; the general theorem deliberately does not make\nthat assumption.\n\nFor deterministic c_0 put R_0=|c_0| and M=max(|u_0|,|v_0|). (23)\n\nThey follow respectively from |c'|<=a, |v'|<=a|c|, and\n|u'|<=a|c||v|, and hold in either direction of feature time. All bounds\nare polynomial in S,M,R_0.\n\nFor completeness, derivative bounds require no control of z_0. Use when the user asks to solve, prove, derive, analyze, verify, or correct a mathematical problem that is not primarily an incremental lesson about supplied technical documents. Infer the strongest reasonable interpretation without inventing assumptions, establish a minimal setup, use minimal notation, expose every nontrivial step, state and verify external theorem hypotheses, give proof architecture when useful, and silently audit the draft before answering.\n---\n\n# Solve Mathematics Rigorously\n\nProduce a complete mathematical answer rather than a staged document lesson, unless the user explicitly requests incremental pacing. Optimize simultaneously for correctness, rigor, clarity, and economy.\n\n## Determine the intended problem\n\nInterpret the strongest reasonable version supported by the wording and mathematical context. Do not exploit an omission to answer a trivial or uninteresting straw-man version.\n\nHandle ambiguity according to its effect:\n\n- If it does not change the substance, adopt the conventional interpretation and state it briefly.\n- If one reasonable assumption makes the intended problem precise, state that assumption before solving.\n- If several reasonable interpretations lead to materially different answers, ask for clarification or give a compact case split when that fully resolves the ambiguity.\n- If a missing assumption is necessary for the claimed conclusion, do not invent it silently.\n\nIf the stated claim is false, say so and give a decisive counterexample or contradiction. Present a corrected nearby statement only when it helps, and label it as a correction rather than the original result.\n\nAnswer the problem actually asked. Do not weaken a universal claim to a convenient special case, replace an exact question with a numerical approximation, or prove a converse instead of the stated direction.\n\n## Establish a minimal setup\n\nBegin with a compact setup or preliminaries passage when it reduces ambiguity or supports later reasoning. State only the necessary:\n\n- domain and assumptions;\n- definitions used in the solution;\n- boundary, regularity, integrability, independence, or nondegeneracy conditions that matter;\n- notation that must persist through the answer.\n\nReuse the problem's notation whenever possible. Introduce a new symbol only when the unabbreviated expression is genuinely cumbersome and recurs often. Define every new symbol before use and state its mathematical type when that is not immediately evident.\n\nDo not force a visible setup heading for a short elementary problem. Do not hide substantive assumptions in prose later in the solution.\n\n## Give the architecture before a substantial proof\n\nBefore a long or conceptually difficult proof, explain the plan in a short paragraph or compact list. Identify the main steps, cases, claims, or lemmas and how they combine to yield the conclusion.\n\nKeep the architecture at the level needed to orient the reader. Do not duplicate the detailed proof, and do not substitute a plan for the proof itself. For a routine calculation or one-step argument, omit the architecture.\n\n## Execute every nontrivial step\n\nDerive each nontrivial algebraic, analytic, probabilistic, geometric, combinatorial, or logical transition. Give enough detail for the user to verify the step without reconstructing missing reasoning.\n\nIn particular:\n\n- identify the definition, identity, inequality, or prior claim being used;\n- show substitutions, rearrangements, case transitions, and changes of variables that carry real mathematical content;\n- track domains, signs, quantifiers, equality conditions, exceptional cases, and possible zero denominators;\n- justify limit exchanges, differentiation or integration under a sign, convergence claims, and optimization steps;\n- distinguish exact arguments from heuristics, approximations, and numerical evidence;\n- show that intermediate claims actually imply the requested conclusion.\n\nAvoid words such as \u201cclearly,\u201d \u201cobviously,\u201d \u201cstandard,\u201d and \u201cit follows\u201d when they replace reasoning. They are acceptable only after the supporting reason has been supplied.\n\nDo not over-explain elementary arithmetic or definitions already established. Compress routine steps while preserving the chain of verification.\n\n## Invoke external results completely\n\nWhenever the solution relies on a nontrivial theorem or external fact:\n\n1. state the exact form needed, including its assumptions and conclusion;\n2. verify each assumption in the present problem;\n3. identify the current objects substituted into the theorem;\n4. state exactly what conclusion the theorem yields here.\n\nDo not merely name a theorem or call a fact standard. Prefer the relevant specialization over an unnecessarily general statement.\n\nVerify a recent, specialized, source-specific, or uncertain result against a primary or authoritative source before relying on it. Cite the source near the claim. Paraphrase the relevant result unless its exact wording matters; quote only a short compliant excerpt when useful.\n\nIf the required theorem is unavailable, uncertain, or does not apply, expose the gap instead of proceeding as though it were valid.\n\n## Use examples and checks proportionately\n\nInclude an example, boundary case, counterexample, or remark only when it clarifies an abstraction, tests a hypothesis, explains a consequence, or exposes why an assumption is needed.\n\nUse low-cost sanity checks when they add confidence, such as:\n\n- substituting the result back into the defining relation;\n- checking dimensions, signs, or units;\n- testing a boundary or small case;\n- comparing with a known special case;\n- deriving the same conclusion by a second short route.\n\nDo not add examples mechanically or let them replace the general argument.\n\n## Keep the presentation compact\n\nUse headings only when they make a substantial solution easier to navigate. Keep notation sparse and stable. Prefer explicit expressions to disposable shorthand.\n\nUse equations to carry mathematical content, not decoration. Avoid formula dumps, decorative summaries, and boxed equations. State the conclusion plainly.\n\nLet the problem's complexity determine the answer length. Preserve every necessary assumption, theorem check, and logical transition, but remove repetition and exposition that does not improve understanding or verification.\n\n## Audit and revise silently\n\nDraft the solution, then review it before sending. Check:\n\n1. Did the solution address the strongest reasonable interpretation rather than a straw man?\n2. Were all adopted assumptions stated, and were none invented merely to force the result?\n3. Was the setup sufficient but minimal?\n4. Was every persistent symbol necessary and defined?\n5. Did a substantial proof receive a useful architecture?\n6. Can the reader verify every nontrivial transition?\n7. Was every external theorem stated in the needed form, with its hypotheses checked and its conclusion applied explicitly?\n8. Were recent, specialized, or uncertain results verified from an authoritative source?\n9. Were edge cases, equality conditions, domains, and quantifiers handled?\n10. Does the final conclusion answer the exact question?\n11. Do examples or remarks earn their space?\n12. Is the answer as compact as rigor allows, with no boxed equations?\n\nRevise any failing part and repeat the audit until the answer satisfies the checklist as well as possible. Do not mention the drafting or audit process in the final answer.\n", "truncated": false}}

Build the claim ladder\n\nSeparate at least:\n\n1. exact identities or finite constructions;\n2. well-posedness or internal correctness of the proposed approximation;\n3. empirical accuracy on tested instances;\n4. convergence of the approximation hierarchy;\n5. identification with the intended limit or real system;\n6. compact-horizon validity;\n7. all-time or uniform validity;\n8. existence of some admissible witness versus success of the current witness.\n\nDo not promote evidence from one rung to another without an explicit bridge.\n\n### 4. State the sharp conjecture\n\nWrite explicit quantifiers and dependencies. State:\n\n- the admissible problem class;\n- what may depend on accuracy, horizon, and regularity;\n- what must remain independent of ambient scale or inaccessible trajectory information;\n- the approximation guarantee and norm;\n- coefficient provenance and initialization;\n- autonomy, restartability, or finite-memory requirements;\n- concrete falsifiers.\n\nIf several statements are needed, identify one central conjecture and label the rest as lemmas, assumptions, or stronger extensions.\n\n### 5. Derive the exact causal skeleton\n\nDerive before truncating. Mark each step as exact, conditional on named assumptions, heuristic, empirical, or open. Track causal dependencies, information flow, history dependence, and the source of every residual.\n\nKeep distinct approximation axes distinct. Convergence in time step, basis degree, perturbation order, response depth, width, spatial resolution, or another coordinate does not imply convergence in any other axis.\n\nIn particular, separate:\n\n- correctness of every fixed-order coefficient from convergence of the full expansion;\n- production of omitted error from propagation or stability of that error;\n- observable accuracy from state-space convergence;\n- failure of a proof route from falsity of the conjecture.\n\n### 6. Build a typed evidence ledger\n\nFor each central claim, record its exact statement, claim-ladder rung, status, assumptions, supporting and contrary evidence, dependencies, cheapest resolver, falsifier, and supersession relation.\n\nUse calibrated labels. Distinguish proved, exact under assumptions, empirically supported, disfavored, falsified, open, and superseded. Never translate \u201cnot observed\u201d into \u201cruled out.\u201d\n\n### 7. Select one bottleneck\n\nRank candidate next steps by logical leverage, expected uncertainty reduction, mechanism relevance, and cost. Prefer the smallest step whose possible outcomes would change the research state.\n\nDo not optimize for volume of algebra, number of experiments, or apparent completeness. Do not accumulate mutually inconsistent report layers.\n\n## Decision rules\n\n- **Strong surrogate performance:** Treat it as evidence of utility, not hierarchy convergence or arbitrary-accuracy existence.\n- **A successful matched nonlinear or causal control:** Downgrade that particular mundane explanation; do not infer a theorem.\n- **A failed high-order trend:** Check parity, conditioning, common-reference comparability, and replication before inferring divergence.\n- **A correct local expansion with growing coefficients:** Preserve the local algebra while rejecting unsupported positive-horizon convergence.\n- **A stability theorem without a source estimate:** Record propagation control; leave error production open.\n- **Failure of one representation:** Distinguish failure of the explicit witness from failure of the broader admissible class.\n- **Confounded evidence:** Mark inconclusive and stop unless a resolution run was preauthorized.\n- **Budget exhausted:** Stop even when the scientific question remains open.\n\n## Output standards\n\nMatch the requested artifact. Source hierarchy\n\nTreat conversation recollection as a locator, not as the technical authority. Prefer:\n\n1. checked derivations, proofs, and raw experimental outputs;\n2. source code, frozen configurations, and provenance manifests;\n3. audited final reports tied to those artifacts;\n4. provisional notes and intermediate reports;\n5. conversational summaries.\n\nRecency alone does not establish correctness. A later statement supersedes an earlier one only when it uses corrected assumptions, better evidence, or a valid argument that changes the conclusion.\n\n## 2. Claim record\n\nMaintain one record per central claim:\n\n```markdown\n### C-<number>: <short name>\n\n- Statement:\n- Claim-ladder rung:\n- Status:\n- Scope and assumptions:\n- Supporting evidence:\n- Contrary evidence:\n- Dependencies:\n- Cheapest decisive resolver:\n- Concrete falsifier:\n- Supersedes:\n- Superseded by:\n- Authoritative sources:\n```\n\nKeep the statement narrow enough that one status applies.\n\n## 3. Status vocabulary\n\nUse one primary status:\n\n| Status | Meaning |\n|---|---|\n| Proved | Established by a complete argument under stated hypotheses |\n| Exact-under-assumptions | Algebraically or logically exact once named assumptions are granted |\n| Empirically-supported | Supported over a specified tested scope |\n| Strongly-disfavored | Conflicts with replicated valid evidence but is not logically impossible |\n| Disfavored | Has meaningful contrary evidence with caveats |\n| Falsified | Contradicted over the exact stated scope |\n| Open | A necessary bridge or discriminator is missing |\n| Inconclusive | The attempted test failed a validity gate or decision threshold |\n| Superseded | Replaced by a more valid formulation or result |\n\nAdd a confidence qualifier only when it clarifies evidence quality; do not substitute confidence for status.\n\n## 4. Supersession protocol\n\nWhen a result changes:\n\n1. Identify the exact old claim.\n2. Identify what changed: statement, assumption, method, data, numerical validity, or interpretation.\n3. Decide whether the old claim is falsified, narrowed, or merely superseded.\n4. Point the old record to the new one.\n5. Use only the new valid conclusion in the main narrative.\n6. Preserve the old record for provenance, not as a competing conclusion.\n\nExamples of legitimate supersession:\n\n- a parity or indexing correction invalidates an old comparison;\n- a well-conditioned replication overturns a numerically confounded trend;\n- a stronger matched control defeats the earlier baseline inference;\n- a proof audit finds a missing hypothesis;\n- a later theorem establishes a previously assumed bridge.\n\nA newer report that merely restates a claim does not supersede its source evidence.\n\n## 5. Contradiction audit\n\nFor apparently conflicting artifacts, compare:\n\n| Dimension | Questions |\n|---|---|\n| Target | Are they answering the same conjecture and observables? |\n| Scope | Are data class, horizon, and limit order identical? |\n| Approximation axis | Are they varying the same cutoff or resolution? |\n| Reference | Do they compare against the same ground truth? |\n| Numerics | Did either fail conditioning or replication gates? |\n| Method | Did one repair a known flaw in the other? |\n| Claim level | Is one empirical while the other asserts convergence? |\n\nOften the correct resolution is two scoped claims, not one winner.\n\n## 6. Causal update\n\nAfter each meaningful result, add:\n\n```markdown\n### Update U-<number>\n\n- New evidence:\n- Validity scope:\n- Mechanism affected:\n- Claims upgraded:\n- Claims downgraded:\n- Claims unchanged and why:\n- Superseded conclusion:\n- Newly exposed dependency:\n- Authorized next branch, if any:\n```\n\nState why unchanged claims do not follow from the result. This prevents a successful experiment from silently upgrading unrelated theorem obligations.\n\n## 7. Research-state summary\n\nMaintain a compact table:\n\n| Category | Contents |\n|---|---|\n| Established | Proofs, exact constructions, and verified identities |\n| Supported | Empirical or conditional claims with stated scope |\n| Disfavored | Alternative explanations with contrary evidence |\n| Falsified | Claims rejected over an exact scope |\n| Open | Necessary unresolved assumptions and proof obligations |\n| Superseded | Old conclusions replaced by corrected ones |\n\nRank open items by logical leverage rather than by ease.\n\n## 8. Master-report protocol\n\nWrite an authoritative report in this order:\n\n1. current bottom line;\n2. canonical contract and sharp conjecture;\n3. exact causal or mathematical skeleton;\n4. strongest theoretical support;\n5. empirical evidence and validity scope;\n6. adversarial alternatives and matched controls;\n7. established, supported, disfavored, falsified, and open claims;\n8. supersession note for materially changed conclusions;\n9. highest-leverage unresolved obligation.\n\nDo not narrate every historical iteration. Include history only when it explains why a conclusion changed or why a tempting argument is invalid.\n\n## 9. Failed-program salvage\n\nWhen an approach fails, inventory separately:\n\n- exact identities that remain valid;\n- useful representations or algorithms;\n- counterexamples and no-go results;\n- causal mechanisms exposed;\n- assumptions shown necessary;\n- conclusions that must be discarded.\n\nFailure of the theorem does not erase valid algebra. Valid algebra does not rescue the failed theorem.\n# Adversarial Audit\n\nUse this reference before calling a result decisive, closing a loophole, declaring a route impossible, or claiming that only technical work remains.\n\n## Audit protocol\n\nFor every central claim or proposed next step, write:\n\n| Item | Question |\n|---|---|\n| Target claim | What exact statement is under attack? |\n| Strongest alternative | What is the most plausible competing explanation or obstruction? |\n| Failure signature | What observation or derivation would distinguish it? |\n| Cheapest discriminator | What is the smallest valid theory or experiment check? |\n| Validity gates | What would make the check numerically or logically uninterpretable? |\n| Consequence | Which claim-ladder rung changes under each outcome? |\n\nAttack the strongest viable alternative. Do not use a weak baseline merely because it is easy to defeat.\n\n## Structural attacks\n\n### Hidden information and memory\n\n- Check whether coefficients, initialization, or forcing use the future target trajectory.\n- Check whether a finite coordinate hides an arbitrary function, history, matrix, dataset, or unbounded-precision code.\n- Check whether the current state determines future evolution or whether untracked response/history variables survive.\n- Test restartability from an intermediate state when autonomy is claimed.\n\n### Nonclosure and tail escape\n\n- Identify the exact residual generated by truncation.\n- Check whether bounded norm implies compactness in the required topology; usually it does not.\n- Check whether high modes can feed low observables through multiplication, contraction, transpose, aliasing, or nonlinear interaction.\n- Distinguish small individual coefficients from a small collective tail.\n- Ask for a reachable-state estimate rather than a bound on an arbitrary ambient ball.\n\n### Error production versus propagation\n\n- Locate where omitted error is produced.\n- Locate the stability or propagator estimate that transports it.\n- Reject arguments that provide only stability but no small source term.\n- Check for nonnormal or transient amplification even when eigenvalues appear stable.\n\n### Limits and topology\n\n- Write the limit order explicitly.\n- Check every interchange of limit, expectation, derivative, integral, optimization, or long-time operation.\n- Verify tightness, uniform integrability, regularity, or compactness assumptions needed for the interchange.\n- Confirm that convergence in the chosen topology controls the named observables.\n- Separate compact-time results from all-time claims.\n\n### Witness versus existence\n\n- Determine whether a failure concerns the chosen basis, closure, solver, or state.\n- Check whether a modest admissible enrichment could repair the witness.\n- Do not use failure of one construction as a no-go theorem for the full approximation class.\n\n## Mundane-explanation attacks\n\nConstruct the strongest scientifically plausible nuisance explanation, such as:\n\n- effective linearity after matching gain, scale, spectrum, or another nuisance parameter;\n- frozen-feature, lazy, low-rank, or small-motion behavior;\n- interpolation rather than autonomous prediction;\n- observable insensitivity despite wrong internal dynamics;\n- a special symmetry or data degeneracy;\n- an easier regime introduced by the testbed.\n\nUse a matched control that grants the nuisance explanation its best legitimate parameters without giving it the target trajectory. Add a mechanism-specific ablation when possible.\n\nClosing a loophole requires evidence against this strongest version, not against identity dynamics or another straw man.\n\n## Numerical and empirical attacks\n\n- Check conditioning before interpreting high-order behavior.\n- Use a common reference when comparing successive approximations.\n- Separate discretization, quadrature, sampling, optimization, and model errors.\n- Replicate any conclusion that is comparable to numerical variability.\n- Freeze primary metrics and thresholds before inspecting the decisive result.\n- Track negative and failed runs, not only favorable configurations.\n- Distinguish a theory-selected stress test from a searched favorable example.\n- Check source hashes, configurations, seeds, and raw-result provenance.\n- Treat failure of a validity gate as inconclusive, not as evidence for either hypothesis.\n\n## Logical severity\n\nClassify each surviving issue:\n\n- **Fatal:** contradicts the central conjecture under its stated contract.\n- **Witness-fatal:** defeats the current construction but not the existence claim.\n- **Major:** blocks a necessary bridge between claim-ladder rungs.\n- **Conditional:** matters only if a named assumption fails.\n- **Minor:** affects constants, presentation, or a noncentral extension.\n\nDo not call a gap \u201ctechnical\u201d unless a known theorem or fully specified argument appears to close it under already verified hypotheses.\n\n## Evidence language\n\nUse these distinctions:\n\n- **Ruled out:** logically contradicted or rejected by a valid, sufficiently powered discriminator over the stated scope.\n- **Strongly disfavored:** survives logically but conflicts with replicated evidence.\n- **Disfavored:** has contrary evidence with meaningful caveats.\n- **Unsupported:** lacks positive evidence.\n- **Open:** viable evidence exists on both sides or the decisive check is missing.\n- **Inconclusive:** the attempted check failed a logical or numerical validity gate.\n\n\u201cNo failure observed\u201d is not \u201cruled out.\u201d \u201cAccurate on tested observables\u201d is not \u201cmechanism identified.\u201d\n\n## Final hostile questions\n\nBefore upgrading the research claim, answer:\n\n1. What is the strongest surviving ordinary explanation?\n2. What is the strongest structural obstruction?\n3. What numerical artifact could mimic the result?\n4. Which necessary claim-ladder bridge remains assumed?\n5. Would the opposite result have changed the conclusion?\n6. Was that decision rule fixed before observing the result?\n7. Does the evidence generalize beyond the selected instances?\n8. What would still make the central claim false?\n# Bounded Multi-Route Proof Search\n\nUse this mode to search for a complete proof of a mathematical conjecture across several genuinely different approaches. Do not treat the mode, the user's optimism, or the conjecture's fame as evidence that a proof exists.\n\n## Fix the proof contract\n\nBefore choosing approaches, record:\n\n- the exact theorem, quantifiers, hypotheses, definitions, and edge cases;\n- which classical or supplied results may be used;\n- what counts as a complete proof;\n- which special cases, reductions, computations, or conditional results do not resolve the target;\n- the time, compute, token, and concurrency budget;\n- the terminal outcomes: a proof that survives audit, or budget exhaustion with exact proved results and gaps.\n\nDo not prohibit literature search unless the user requests an independent-discovery or benchmark-integrity mode. Do not assume an affirmative proof exists unless the user explicitly poses a hypothetical; even then, accept only a complete audited argument.\n\n## Choose the execution mode\n\nUse delegated or parallel workers only when the user explicitly authorizes delegation or parallel work and the environment supports it. Otherwise explore the routes sequentially with the same records and stopping rules. Never hard-code an agent count, runtime, or product-specific orchestration feature.\n\nAllocate available effort dynamically. Match the portfolio size to the budget, keep synthesis capacity in reserve, and reduce breadth when routes cannot be audited adequately.\n\n## Seed a diverse portfolio\n\nDefine approach families by mathematical mechanism, not wording. Choose only families appropriate to the theorem, such as structural induction, extremal arguments, algebraic or analytic reformulation, probabilistic construction, duality, invariants, decomposition, topology, optimization, or computation-assisted discovery.\n\nDuring the first round, preserve independence:\n\n- give each route the same proof contract without presenting a favored strategy as established;\n- avoid duplicating superficially different versions of one mechanism;\n- keep incompatible approaches alive long enough to expose their real strengths and obstructions;\n- reserve cross-pollination until routes have produced concrete mathematics.\n\n## Maintain an approach registry\n\nKeep one row per route:\n\n| Field | Record |\n|---|---|\n| Route | Stable identifier and approach family |\n| Mechanism | Proposed reason the route could prove the theorem |\n| Deliverable | Lemma, construction, equation, reduction, or counterexample sought |\n| Dependencies | External theorems and unproved internal claims |\n| Strongest result | Exact statement currently established |\n| Obstacle | Smallest unresolved step and its logical strength |\n| Distinctiveness | How the route differs from active alternatives |\n| Status | Active, promising, blocked, refuted, merged, or complete |\n| Reopen condition | New mechanism, lemma, or evidence required |\n\nUpdate the registry after every round. Cluster routes that use the same mathematical idea even when their notation differs.\n\n## Enforce a concrete route contract\n\nRequire every route to return:\n\n1. a precise claim or construction;\n2. a derivation or proof of everything asserted;\n3. every external dependency and its checked hypotheses;\n4. a counterexample search or sanity check when applicable;\n5. the exact unresolved implication, if any;\n6. a registry-status recommendation and reason.\n\nReject vague progress reports, unsupported optimism, and claims that a global compatibility step is routine. A reduction is meaningful progress only when its target is already proved, strictly better understood, or accompanied by a new mechanism that attacks it.\n\n## Run adaptive rounds\n\nAfter each round:\n\n1. verify concrete outputs before treating them as progress;\n2. merge duplicate routes by mechanism;\n3. identify reductions whose missing lemma is equivalent in strength to the original theorem;\n4. redirect effort toward underexplored families or newly enabled bottlenecks;\n5. mark a route blocked when it stalls at a theorem-strength lemma;\n6. reopen a blocked route only after a materially new invariant, construction, theorem, or counterexample appears;\n7. cross-pollinate mature routes when one route supplies another's missing ingredient;\n8. preserve valid lemmas and counterexamples from failed routes.\n\nDo not let elegance, early popularity, or the amount of work already invested determine allocation.\n\n## Audit candidate proofs independently\n\nWhen a complete route appears, stop expanding it and begin reconstruction. Assign separate checks, sequentially or through authorized workers, to:\n\n- reconstruct every implication from the proof contract;\n- verify all cited theorem statements and hypotheses;\n- test definitions, degenerate cases, boundary cases, and well-definedness;\n- search for circular dependence or an equivalent-strength hidden lemma;\n- generate the theorem-specific failure checklist;\n- seek a minimal counterexample to every vulnerable intermediate claim;\n- distinguish a complete proof from a plausible proof sketch.\n\nIntegrate the final mathematical argument using the rigorous proof workflow. Label it `candidate proof` until the full chain survives independent audit, and state whether any formal or machine verification was actually performed.\n\n## Stop and report honestly\n\nStop when a complete proof survives the authorized audit, or when the hard budget is exhausted. At exhaustion, report the registry, all proved lemmas, refuted and blocked routes, exact remaining gaps, and each route's reopen condition. Never convert persistence instructions into permission to fabricate a proof or conceal an unresolved implication.\n", "truncated": false}}

Master-report protocol\n\nWrite an authoritative report in this order:\n\n1. current bottom line;\n2. canonical contract and sharp conjecture;\n3. exact causal or mathematical skeleton;\n4. strongest theoretical support;\n5. empirical evidence and validity scope;\n6. adversarial alternatives and matched controls;\n7. established, supported, disfavored, falsified, and open claims;\n8. supersession note for materially changed conclusions;\n9. highest-leverage unresolved obligation.\n\nDo not narrate every historical iteration. Include history only when it explains why a conclusion changed or why a tempting argument is invalid.\n\n## 9. Failed-program salvage\n\nWhen an approach fails, inventory separately:\n\n- exact identities that remain valid;\n- useful representations or algorithms;\n- counterexamples and no-go results;\n- causal mechanisms exposed;\n- assumptions shown necessary;\n- conclusions that must be discarded.\n\nFailure of the theorem does not erase valid algebra. Valid algebra does not rescue the failed theorem.\n", "truncated": false}}
TOOL {"type": "commandExecution", "id": "exec-1ec8dcea-9830-4f13-b214-7482ab676c7c", "command": "/bin/bash -lc 'cat /etc/codex/skills/investigate-conjectures/references/adversarial-audit.md'", "cwd": "/home/amir/Codes/PDE", "status": "completed", "exitCode": 0, "durationMs": 0, "output": {"text": "# Adversarial Audit\n\nUse this reference before calling a result decisive, closing a loophole, declaring a route impossible, or claiming that only technical work remains.\n\n## Audit protocol\n\nFor every central claim or proposed next step, write:\n\n| Item | Question |\n|---|---|\n| Target claim | What exact statement is under attack? |\n| Strongest alternative | What is the most plausible competing explanation or obstruction? |\n| Failure signature | What observation or derivation would distinguish it? |\n| Cheapest discriminator | What is the smallest valid theory or experiment check? |\n| Validity gates | What would make the check numerically or logically uninterpretable? |\n| Consequence | Which claim-ladder rung changes under each outcome? |\n\nAttack the strongest viable alternative. Do not use a weak baseline merely because it is easy to defeat.\n\n## Structural attacks\n\n### Hidden information and memory\n\n- Check whether coefficients, initialization, or forcing use the future target trajectory.\n- Check whether a finite coordinate hides an arbitrary function, history, matrix, dataset, or unbounded-precision code.\n- Check whether the current state determines future evolution or whether untracked response/history variables survive.\n- Test restartability from an intermediate state when autonomy is claimed.\n\n### Nonclosure and tail escape\n\n- Identify the exact residual generated by truncation.\n- Check whether bounded norm implies compactness in the required topology; usually it does not.\n- Check whether high modes can feed low observables through multiplication, contraction, transpose, aliasing, or nonlinear interaction.\n- Distinguish small individual coefficients from a small collective tail.\n- Ask for a reachable-state estimate rather than a bound on an arbitrary ambient ball.\n\n### Error production versus propagation\n\n- Locate where omitted error is produced.\n- Locate the stability or propagator estimate that transports it.\n- Reject arguments that provide only stability but no small source term.\n- Check for nonnormal or transient amplification even when eigenvalues appear stable.\n\n### Limits and topology\n\n- Write the limit order explicitly.\n- Check every interchange of limit, expectation, derivative, integral, optimization, or long-time operation.\n- Verify tightness, uniform integrability, regularity, or compactness assumptions needed for the interchange.\n- Confirm that convergence in the chosen topology controls the named observables.\n- Separate compact-time results from all-time claims.\n\n### Witness versus existence\n\n- Determine whether a failure concerns the chosen basis, closure, solver, or state.\n- Check whether a modest admissible enrichment could repair the witness.\n- Do not use failure of one construction as a no-go theorem for the full approximation class.\n\n## Mundane-explanation attacks\n\nConstruct the strongest scientifically plausible nuisance explanation, such as:\n\n- effective linearity after matching gain, scale, spectrum, or another nuisance parameter;\n- frozen-feature, lazy, low-rank, or small-motion behavior;\n- interpolation rather than autonomous prediction;\n- observable insensitivity despite wrong internal dynamics;\n- a special symmetry or data degeneracy;\n- an easier regime introduced by the testbed.\n\nUse a matched control that grants the nuisance explanation its best legitimate parameters without giving it the target trajectory. Add a mechanism-specific ablation when possible.\n\nClosing a loophole requires evidence against this strongest version, not against identity dynamics or another straw man.\n\n## Numerical and empirical attacks\n\n- Check conditioning before interpreting high-order behavior.\n- Use a common reference when comparing successive approximations.\n- Separate discretization, quadrature, sampling, optimization, and model errors.\n- Replicate any conclusion that is comparable to numerical variability.\n- Freeze primary metrics and thresholds before inspecting the decisive result.\n- Track negative and failed runs, not only favorable configurations.\n- Distinguish a theory-selected stress test from a searched favorable example.\n- Check source hashes, configurations, seeds, and raw-result provenance.\n- Treat failure of a validity gate as inconclusive, not as evidence for either hypothesis.\n\n## Logical severity\n\nClassify each surviving issue:\n\n- **Fatal:** contradicts the central conjecture under its stated contract.\n- **Witness-fatal:** defeats the current construction but not the existence claim.\n- **Major:** blocks a necessary bridge between claim-ladder rungs.\n- **Conditional:** matters only if a named assumption fails.\n- **Minor:** affects constants, presentation, or a noncentral extension.\n\nDo not call a gap \u201ctechnical\u201d unless a known theorem or fully specified argument appears to close it under already verified hypotheses.\n\n## Evidence language\n\nUse these distinctions:\n\n- **Ruled out:** logically contradicted or rejected by a valid, sufficiently powered discriminator over the stated scope.\n- **Strongly disfavored:** survives logically but conflicts with replicated evidence.\n- **Disfavored:** has contrary evidence with meaningful caveats.\n- **Unsupported:** lacks positive evidence.\n- **Open:** viable evidence exists on both sides or the decisive check is missing.\n- **Inconclusive:** the attempted check failed a logical or numerical validity gate.\n\n\u201cNo failure observed\u201d is not \u201cruled out.\u201d \u201cAccurate on tested observables\u201d is not \u201cmechanism identified.\u201d\n\n## Final hostile questions\n\nBefore upgrading the research claim, answer:\n\n1. What is the strongest surviving ordinary explanation?\n2. What is the strongest structural obstruction?\n3. What numerical artifact could mimic the result?\n4. Which necessary claim-ladder bridge remains assumed?\n5. Would the opposite result have changed the conclusion?\n6. Was that decision rule fixed before observing the result?\n7. Does the evidence generalize beyond the selected instances?\n8. What would still make the central claim false?\n", "truncated": false}}

Do not treat the mode, the user's optimism, or the conjecture's fame as evidence that a proof exists.\n\n## Fix the proof contract\n\nBefore choosing approaches, record:\n\n- the exact theorem, quantifiers, hypotheses, definitions, and edge cases;\n- which classical or supplied results may be used;\n- what counts as a complete proof;\n- which special cases, reductions, computations, or conditional results do not resolve the target;\n- the time, compute, token, and concurrency budget;\n- the terminal outcomes: a proof that survives audit, or budget exhaustion with exact proved results and gaps.\n\nDo not prohibit literature search unless the user requests an independent-discovery or benchmark-integrity mode. Do not assume an affirmative proof exists unless the user explicitly poses a hypothetical; even then, accept only a complete audited argument.\n\n## Choose the execution mode\n\nUse delegated or parallel workers only when the user explicitly authorizes delegation or parallel work and the environment supports it. Otherwise explore the routes sequentially with the same records and stopping rules. Never hard-code an agent count, runtime, or product-specific orchestration feature.\n\nAllocate available effort dynamically. Match the portfolio size to the budget, keep synthesis capacity in reserve, and reduce breadth when routes cannot be audited adequately.\n\n## Seed a diverse portfolio\n\nDefine approach families by mathematical mechanism, not wording. Choose only families appropriate to the theorem, such as structural induction, extremal arguments, algebraic or analytic reformulation, probabilistic construction, duality, invariants, decomposition, topology, optimization, or computation-assisted discovery.\n\nDuring the first round, preserve independence:\n\n- give each route the same proof contract without presenting a favored strategy as established;\n- avoid duplicating superficially different versions of one mechanism;\n- keep incompatible approaches alive long enough to expose their real strengths and obstructions;\n- reserve cross-pollination until routes have produced concrete mathematics.\n\n## Maintain an approach registry\n\nKeep one row per route:\n\n| Field | Record |\n|---|---|\n| Route | Stable identifier and approach family |\n| Mechanism | Proposed reason the route could prove the theorem |\n| Deliverable | Lemma, construction, equation, reduction, or counterexample sought |\n| Dependencies | External theorems and unproved internal claims |\n| Strongest result | Exact statement currently established |\n| Obstacle | Smallest unresolved step and its logical strength |\n| Distinctiveness | How the route differs from active alternatives |\n| Status | Active, promising, blocked, refuted, merged, or complete |\n| Reopen condition | New mechanism, lemma, or evidence required |\n\nUpdate the registry after every round. Cluster routes that use the same mathematical idea even when their notation differs.\n\n## Enforce a concrete route contract\n\nRequire every route to return:\n\n1. a precise claim or construction;\n2. a derivation or proof of everything asserted;\n3. every external dependency and its checked hypotheses;\n4. a counterexample search or sanity check when applicable;\n5. the exact unresolved implication, if any;\n6. a registry-status recommendation and reason.\n\nReject vague progress reports, unsupported optimism, and claims that a global compatibility step is routine. A reduction is meaningful progress only when its target is already proved, strictly better understood, or accompanied by a new mechanism that attacks it.\n\n## Run adaptive rounds\n\nAfter each round:\n\n1. verify concrete outputs before treating them as progress;\n2. merge duplicate routes by mechanism;\n3. identify reductions whose missing lemma is equivalent in strength to the original theorem;\n4. redirect effort toward underexplored families or newly enabled bottlenecks;\n5. mark a route blocked when it stalls at a theorem-strength lemma;\n6. reopen a blocked route only after a materially new invariant, construction, theorem, or counterexample appears;\n7. cross-pollinate mature routes when one route supplies another's missing ingredient;\n8. preserve valid lemmas and counterexamples from failed routes.\n\nDo not let elegance, early popularity, or the amount of work already invested determine allocation.\n\n## Audit candidate proofs independently\n\nWhen a complete route appears, stop expanding it and begin reconstruction. Assign separate checks, sequentially or through authorized workers, to:\n\n- reconstruct every implication from the proof contract;\n- verify all cited theorem statements and hypotheses;\n- test definitions, degenerate cases, boundary cases, and well-definedness;\n- search for circular dependence or an equivalent-strength hidden lemma;\n- generate the theorem-specific failure checklist;\n- seek a minimal counterexample to every vulnerable intermediate claim;\n- distinguish a complete proof from a plausible proof sketch.\n\nIntegrate the final mathematical argument using the rigorous proof workflow. Label it `candidate proof` until the full chain survives independent audit, and state whether any formal or machine verification was actually performed.\n\n## Stop and report honestly\n\nStop when a complete proof survives the authorized audit, or when the hard budget is exhausted. At exhaustion, report the registry, all proved lemmas, refuted and blocked routes, exact remaining gaps, and each route's reopen condition. Never convert persistence instructions into permission to fabricate a proof or conceal an unresolved implication.\n", "truncated": false}}

It is not a trajectory of the canonical Gaussian network and does not refute a network-specific localization theorem.\n\nLet a probability space contain a set E of measure epsilon in (0,1/2], and put\n\n    u = 1_E / sqrt(epsilon),\n    v = 1_(E^c) / sqrt(1-epsilon).\n\nThese vectors are orthonormal in normalized L2. In particular A(u+v)=v.\n\nFor t in a fixed interval [0,S], set\n\n    delta(t) = t(u+v),\n    z(t) = (t^2/2)v,\n    D(z) = 1/(1+z^2),\n    q(t) = (1+z(t)^2) delta(t).\n\nAll products and the definition of D are pointwise. Since u and v have disjoint support,\n\n    q(t) = t u + [t + t^5/(4(1-epsilon))]v,\n    q'(t) = u + [1 + 5t^4/(4(1-epsilon))]v.\n\nThus delta = D(z)q and the required equation holds exactly:\n\n    z'(t) = t v = A delta(t) = A D(z(t))q(t).\n\nBoth z(0) and q(0) are zero; all trajectories are smooth. The same is true of the operator A and all its time derivatives, since A is constant.\n\n## Failure of uniform integrability\n\nFor each fixed t > 0,\n\n    integral_E |delta(t)|^2 = t^2,\n\nalthough measure(E)=epsilon tends to zero. Therefore the squared gated fields are not uniformly integrable at any positive fixed time. They are not uniformly integrable in space-time either, because\n\n    measure([0,S] x E) = S epsilon -> 0,\n    integral_0^S integral_E |delta(t)|^2 dt = S^3/3 > 0.\n\nOn E the cancellation is exact: (A delta)|_E=0, hence z|_E=0 and D(z)|_E=1. Positivity controls the total pairing <delta,A delta>, not its restriction to E. The off-diagonal contribution cancels the positive rare-coordinate contribution and transfers motion to the bulk.\n\n## Instantaneous algebraic factorization\n\nEven the algebraic form A = m_1 I + W D_1^2 W* does not by itself rule this out. This factorization respects the arctan relation between h_1 and D_1, but it does NOT enforce z=W h_1, a Gaussian law for W, learned-weight flow equations, or the coupled evolution of z_1. It is an algebraic observation only.\n\nIf a zero initial derivative q'(0) is also desired, replace t in delta by t^2 and replace t^2/2 in z by t^3/3. The same argument and uniform bounds on every fixed interval hold, and both q(0) and q'(0) then vanish.\n\nConclusion: uniform operator positivity, bounded normalized-L2 q and q', bounded normalized-L2 z, zero initial q, and bounded total action do not imply uniform integrability of |D(z)q|^2. Additional structure from canonical Gaussian initialization and the exact coupled dynamics is essential.\n", "truncated": false}}

(1)\n\nFor a fixed hidden tangent `u=(u1,E2,E3)`, define\n\n    T1 u=u1,\n    T2 u=E2 h1/sqrt(n)+W2 D1 u1,\n    T3 u=E3 h2/sqrt(n)+W3 D2 T2 u,\n    S2 u=E2^T delta2/sqrt(n),\n    S3 u=E3^T delta3/sqrt(n).\n\nThe derivatives of these operators, evaluated on the same fixed `u`,\nare exactly\n\n    T1'=0,\n    T2' u=E2 D1 nu1/sqrt(n)+W2' D1 u1+W2 D1' u1,\n    T3' u=E3 D2 nu2/sqrt(n)+W3' D2 T2 u\n                         +W3 D2' T2 u+W3 D2 T2' u,\n    S2' u=E2^T delta2'/sqrt(n),\n    S3' u=E3^T delta3'/sqrt(n),\n    J'=D3' T3+D3 T3'.                               (2)\n\nPut\n\n    M1=diag(phi''(z1) q1),\n    M2=diag(phi''(z2) q2),\n    M3=diag(phi''(z3) c).\n\nTheir full derivatives are\n\n    M1'=diag(phi'''(z1) nu1 q1+phi''(z1) q1'),\n    M2'=diag(phi'''(z2) nu2 q2+phi''(z2) q2'),\n    M3'=diag(phi'''(z3) nu3 c+phi''(z3) h3).          (3)\n\nFor `sym K=K+K^T`, direct differentiation of the complete Hessian gives\n\n    A'=sum_(l=1)^3 [ (T_l')^T M_l T_l\n                    +T_l^T M_l' T_l+T_l^T M_l T_l' ]\n       +sym[ (S2')^T D1 T1+S2^T D1' T1 ]\n       +sym[ (S3')^T D2 T2+S3^T D2' T2+S3^T D2 T2' ],\n\n    B'=[A' (J')^T; J' 0]=D^3 F[b].                 (4)\n\nEquations (1)-(4) include both trained matrices, all differentiation\nthrough their transposes, readout training, and all mixed Hessian terms.\nNo backward query has been frozen. For a full tangent `U=(u,v)`, they give\n\n    U^T(B'-kappa B^2)U\n       =u^T A' u+2v^T J' u\n          -kappa (||Au+J^T v||^2+||Ju||^2).         (5)\n\n## 2. Explicit bounded-primal canonical state\n\nTake any even width `n>=2`. Let `1` be the all-ones vector, let `e` be a\nunit vector with `n/2` entries `1/sqrt(n)` and `n/2` entries\n`-1/sqrt(n)`, and let `e1,e2` be the first two coordinate vectors. Put\n\n    a=pi/4,   m=a^2<1,\n    v=e1-2e2,   w=e1+e2,\n    z1=1,   W2=2 w e^T,   W3=1 v^T/sqrt(n),   c=1.  (6)\n\nThis is an actual state of the stated three-hidden-layer architecture.\nIts primal quantities satisfy\n\n    ||W2||op=2sqrt(2),   ||W3||op=sqrt(5),\n    ||c||/sqrt(n)=1,   ||z1||/sqrt(n)=1,\n    h1=a1,   z2=z3=h2=h3=0.\n\nIn particular every readout coordinate is bounded; only RMS bounds are\nneeded. The backward fields are\n\n    delta3=1,  q2=delta2=sqrt(n)v,\n    q1=-2sqrt(n)e,  delta1=-sqrt(n)e.                (7)\n\nThus both backward RMS bounds hold, but `q2` has two concentrated\ncoordinates. Using the actual trained-weight velocities gives\n\n    W2'=a v1^T/sqrt(n),    W3'=0,    c'=0,\n    nu1=-sqrt(n)e,\n    nu2=sqrt(n)(m v-w),\n    nu3=(5m+1)1,\n    delta3'=q2'=delta2'=0,   q1'=5a1.               (8)\n\nFor example, `W2' h1=m sqrt(n)v`, while\n`W2 D1 nu1=-sqrt(n)w`; their sum yields `nu2`. Thus `q2_1>0`\nbut `nu2_1<0`. No such bound holds for its material derivative.\n\n## 3. Exact positive Rayleigh quotient\n\nChoose the unit hidden variation and the corresponding unit full\nvariation\n\n    u1=0,   E2=e1 1^T/sqrt(n),   E3=0,   U=(u,0).\n\nEquations (2), (6), and the balance of `e` give\n\n    T1u=0,   T2u=a e1,   T2'u=0,\n    T3u=a1/sqrt(n),   S3u=0.\n\nIn the contraction `u^T A' u`, every bottom-layer and mixed term of\n(4) vanishes exactly: the second-layer mixed terms contain `T1u=0`,\nand the third-layer mixed terms contain `S3u=S3'u=0`. (10)\n\nFor every fixed real `kappa`, this tends to positive infinity.\nIn particular the proposed `kappa=3/2` form is\n\n    2m(1-m)n-10m^2-5m-3/8.                         (11)\n\nNo constant depending only on the stated primal bounds can upper-bound\n`B'-(3/2)B^2` as a quadratic form. Optimize simultaneously for correctness, rigor, clarity, and economy.\n\n## Determine the intended problem\n\nInterpret the strongest reasonable version supported by the wording and mathematical context. Do not exploit an omission to answer a trivial or uninteresting straw-man version.\n\nHandle ambiguity according to its effect:\n\n- If it does not change the substance, adopt the conventional interpretation and state it briefly.\n- If one reasonable assumption makes the intended problem precise, state that assumption before solving.\n- If several reasonable interpretations lead to materially different answers, ask for clarification or give a compact case split when that fully resolves the ambiguity.\n- If a missing assumption is necessary for the claimed conclusion, do not invent it silently.\n\nIf the stated claim is false, say so and give a decisive counterexample or contradiction. Paraphrase the relevant result unless its exact wording matters; quote only a short compliant excerpt when useful.\n\nIf the required theorem is unavailable, uncertain, or does not apply, expose the gap instead of proceeding as though it were valid.\n\n## Use examples and checks proportionately\n\nInclude an example, boundary case, counterexample, or remark only when it clarifies an abstraction, tests a hypothesis, explains a consequence, or exposes why an assumption is needed.\n\nUse low-cost sanity checks when they add confidence, such as:\n\n- substituting the result back into the defining relation;\n- checking dimensions, signs, or units;\n- testing a boundary or small case;\n- comparing with a known special case;\n- deriving the same conclusion by a second short route.\n\nDo not add examples mechanically or let them replace the general argument.\n\n## Keep the presentation compact\n\nUse headings only when they make a substantial solution easier to navigate. Do not mention the drafting or audit process in the final answer.\n---\nname: investigate-conjectures\ndescription: Develop, refine, and adversarially test open mathematical, computational, or mechanistic conjectures through bounded theory\u2013experiment and multi-route proof-search programs. Restate the research target in one sentence.\n2. Restore the authoritative state\n\nRead the current primary artifacts before reasoning from memory. Identify the canonical statement, latest valid methods, raw evidence, unresolved objections, and superseded conclusions. Prefer derivations and raw results over summaries; prefer audited later results only when they actually repair or invalidate earlier work.\n\nWhen resuming or synthesizing, maintain the ledger in [evidence-ledger.md](references/evidence-ledger.md).\n\n### 2. Freeze the research contract\n\nSpecify the canonical object, admissible inputs, observables, error criterion, topology or norm, horizon, limit order, approximation family, coefficient provenance, complexity dependence, and forbidden substitutions. State what information the proposed surrogate may retain and whether it must be autonomous and restartable.\n\nApply the non-vacuity checks in [research-contract.md](references/research-contract.md). Reject oracle playback, hidden encodings of the target trajectory, replacement by an easier model, or quantifiers that avoid the intended compression problem.\n\n### 3. Build the claim ladder\n\nSeparate at least:\n\n1. exact identities or finite constructions;\n2. well-posedness or internal correctness of the proposed approximation;\n3. empirical accuracy on tested instances;\n4. convergence of the approximation hierarchy;\n5. identification with the intended limit or real system;\n6. compact-horizon validity;\n7. all-time or uniform validity;\n8. existence of some admissible witness versus success of the current witness.\n\nDo not promote evidence from one rung to another without an explicit bridge.\n\n### 4. State the sharp conjecture\n\nWrite explicit quantifiers and dependencies. State:\n\n- the admissible problem class;\n- what may depend on accuracy, horizon, and regularity;\n- what must remain independent of ambient scale or inaccessible trajectory information;\n- the approximation guarantee and norm;\n- coefficient provenance and initialization;\n- autonomy, restartability, or finite-memory requirements;\n- concrete falsifiers.\n\nIf several statements are needed, identify one central conjecture and label the rest as lemmas, assumptions, or stronger extensions.\n\n### 5. Derive the exact causal skeleton\n\nDerive before truncating. Mark each step as exact, conditional on named assumptions, heuristic, empirical, or open. Track causal dependencies, information flow, history dependence, and the source of every residual.\n\nKeep distinct approximation axes distinct. Convergence in time step, basis degree, perturbation order, response depth, width, spatial resolution, or another coordinate does not imply convergence in any other axis.\n\nIn particular, separate:\n\n- correctness of every fixed-order coefficient from convergence of the full expansion;\n- production of omitted error from propagation or stability of that error;\n- observable accuracy from state-space convergence;\n- failure of a proof route from falsity of the conjecture.\n\n### 6. Build a typed evidence ledger\n\nFor each central claim, record its exact statement, claim-ladder rung, status, assumptions, supporting and contrary evidence, dependencies, cheapest resolver, falsifier, and supersession relation.\n\nUse calibrated labels. Distinguish proved, exact under assumptions, empirically supported, disfavored, falsified, open, and superseded. Never translate \u201cnot observed\u201d into \u201cruled out.\u201d\n\n### 7. Run a hostile audit\n\nAttack the strongest plausible version of each alternative, not a straw man. Check for:\n\n- hidden memory or inaccessible information;\n- tail escape and high-to-low feedback;\n- topology or observable mismatch;\n- nonnormal amplification and unstable error propagation;\n- unjustified interchange of limits;\n- easier-model substitution;\n- nuisance explanations matched only by weak controls;\n- numerical conditioning, discretization, leakage, or selection effects;\n- a result that validates one witness but not the intended existence claim.\n\nUse [adversarial-audit.md](references/adversarial-audit.md) and state what each surviving objection would change.\n\n### 8. Select one bottleneck\n\nRank candidate next steps by logical leverage, expected uncertainty reduction, mechanism relevance, and cost. Prefer the smallest step whose possible outcomes would change the research state.\n\nDo not optimize for volume of algebra, number of experiments, or apparent completeness. Avoid polishing downstream machinery while an upstream identification or compactness gap remains decisive.\n\nNormally pursue one bottleneck at a time. In an authorized multi-route proof search, retain a small structurally diverse portfolio and select one explicit bottleneck per active route using [proof-search-orchestration.md](references/proof-search-orchestration.md).\n\n### 9. Precommit any authorized experiment\n\nBefore implementation or execution, read [decisive-experiments.md](references/decisive-experiments.md) and freeze:\n\n- competing hypotheses;\n- the disputed mechanism and why the testbed preserves it;\n- primary metric and matched controls;\n- pass, fail, and inconclusive thresholds;\n- numerical validity gates;\n- replication rule and conditional branches;\n- exact budget and terminal stopping condition.\n\nUse the smallest nondegenerate testbed, not automatically the smallest instance. Do not launch exploratory grids when one theory-selected stress or ablation can discriminate the hypotheses.\n\n### 10. Update causally\n\nAfter a derivation or experiment, ask:\n\n1. What mechanism was supported, rejected, or left unresolved?\n2. Which exact ledger claims change status?\n3. Does a failure kill the broad conjecture, the current witness, one assumption, or only one proof route?\n4. Which earlier conclusion is superseded?\n5. Does the result authorize a precommitted branch? If not, stop.\n\nSalvage valid identities, tools, counterexamples, and mechanisms from failed programs. Do not preserve their invalid conclusion.\n\n### 11. Synthesize the current state\n\nLead with the strongest defensible conclusion. Report separately:\n\n- established results;\n- supported but unproved claims;\n- disfavored or falsified explanations;\n- unresolved decisive gaps;\n- superseded conclusions;\n- the single highest-leverage next action, if requested and authorized.\n\nWhen later work corrects earlier work, use the corrected result in the main narrative and record the supersession once. Do not accumulate mutually inconsistent report layers.\n\n## Decision rules\n\n- **Strong surrogate performance:** Treat it as evidence of utility, not hierarchy convergence or arbitrary-accuracy existence.\n- **A successful matched nonlinear or causal control:** Downgrade that particular mundane explanation; do not infer a theorem.\n- **A failed high-order trend:** Check parity, conditioning, common-reference comparability, and replication before inferring divergence.\n- **A correct local expansion with growing coefficients:** Preserve the local algebra while rejecting unsupported positive-horizon convergence.\n- **A stability theorem without a source estimate:** Record propagation control; leave error production open.\n- **Failure of one representation:** Distinguish failure of the explicit witness from failure of the broader admissible class.\n- **Confounded evidence:** Mark inconclusive and stop unless a resolution run was preauthorized.\n- **Budget exhausted:** Stop even when the scientific question remains open.\n\n## Output standards\n\nMatch the requested artifact. For a concise assessment, provide the current conclusion, decisive evidence, and leading gap.
