Source hierarchy\n\nTreat conversation recollection as a locator, not as the technical authority. Prefer:\n\n1. checked derivations, proofs, and raw experimental outputs;\n2. source code, frozen configurations, and provenance manifests;\n3. audited final reports tied to those artifacts;\n4. provisional notes and intermediate reports;\n5. conversational summaries.\n\nRecency alone does not establish correctness. A later statement supersedes an earlier one only when it uses corrected assumptions, better evidence, or a valid argument that changes the conclusion.\n\n## 2. Claim record\n\nMaintain one record per central claim:\n\n```markdown\n### C-<number>: <short name>\n\n- Statement:\n- Claim-ladder rung:\n- Status:\n- Scope and assumptions:\n- Supporting evidence:\n- Contrary evidence:\n- Dependencies:\n- Cheapest decisive resolver:\n- Concrete falsifier:\n- Supersedes:\n- Superseded by:\n- Authoritative sources:\n```\n\nKeep the statement narrow enough that one status applies.\n\n## 3. Status vocabulary\n\nUse one primary status:\n\n| Status | Meaning |\n|---|---|\n| Proved | Established by a complete argument under stated hypotheses |\n| Exact-under-assumptions | Algebraically or logically exact once named assumptions are granted |\n| Empirically-supported | Supported over a specified tested scope |\n| Strongly-disfavored | Conflicts with replicated valid evidence but is not logically impossible |\n| Disfavored | Has meaningful contrary evidence with caveats |\n| Falsified | Contradicted over the exact stated scope |\n| Open | A necessary bridge or discriminator is missing |\n| Inconclusive | The attempted test failed a validity gate or decision threshold |\n| Superseded | Replaced by a more valid formulation or result |\n\nAdd a confidence qualifier only when it clarifies evidence quality; do not substitute confidence for status.\n\n## 4. Supersession protocol\n\nWhen a result changes:\n\n1. Identify the exact old claim.\n2. Identify what changed: statement, assumption, method, data, numerical validity, or interpretation.\n3. Decide whether the old claim is falsified, narrowed, or merely superseded.\n4. Point the old record to the new one.\n5. Use only the new valid conclusion in the main narrative.\n6. Preserve the old record for provenance, not as a competing conclusion.\n\nExamples of legitimate supersession:\n\n- a parity or indexing correction invalidates an old comparison;\n- a well-conditioned replication overturns a numerically confounded trend;\n- a stronger matched control defeats the earlier baseline inference;\n- a proof audit finds a missing hypothesis;\n- a later theorem establishes a previously assumed bridge.\n\nA newer report that merely restates a claim does not supersede its source evidence.\n\n## 5. Contradiction audit\n\nFor apparently conflicting artifacts, compare:\n\n| Dimension | Questions |\n|---|---|\n| Target | Are they answering the same conjecture and observables? |\n| Scope | Are data class, horizon, and limit order identical? |\n| Approximation axis | Are they varying the same cutoff or resolution? |\n| Reference | Do they compare against the same ground truth? |\n| Numerics | Did either fail conditioning or replication gates? |\n| Method | Did one repair a known flaw in the other? |\n| Claim level | Is one empirical while the other asserts convergence? |\n\nOften the correct resolution is two scoped claims, not one winner.\n\n## 6. Causal update\n\nAfter each meaningful result, add:\n\n```markdown\n### Update U-<number>\n\n- New evidence:\n- Validity scope:\n- Mechanism affected:\n- Claims upgraded:\n- Claims downgraded:\n- Claims unchanged and why:\n- Superseded conclusion:\n- Newly exposed dependency:\n- Authorized next branch, if any:\n```\n\nState why unchanged claims do not follow from the result. This prevents a successful experiment from silently upgrading unrelated theorem obligations.\n\n## 7. Research-state summary\n\nMaintain a compact table:\n\n| Category | Contents |\n|---|---|\n| Established | Proofs, exact constructions, and verified identities |\n| Supported | Empirical or conditional claims with stated scope |\n| Disfavored | Alternative explanations with contrary evidence |\n| Falsified | Claims rejected over an exact scope |\n| Open | Necessary unresolved assumptions and proof obligations |\n| Superseded | Old conclusions replaced by corrected ones |\n\nRank open items by logical leverage rather than by ease.\n\n## 8. Master-report protocol\n\nWrite an authoritative report in this order:\n\n1. current bottom line;\n2. canonical contract and sharp conjecture;\n3. exact causal or mathematical skeleton;\n4. strongest theoretical support;\n5. empirical evidence and validity scope;\n6. adversarial alternatives and matched controls;\n7. established, supported, disfavored, falsified, and open claims;\n8. supersession note for materially changed conclusions;\n9. highest-leverage unresolved obligation.\n\nDo not narrate every historical iteration. Include history only when it explains why a conclusion changed or why a tempting argument is invalid.\n\n## 9. Failed-program salvage\n\nWhen an approach fails, inventory separately:\n\n- exact identities that remain valid;\n- useful representations or algorithms;\n- counterexamples and no-go results;\n- causal mechanisms exposed;\n- assumptions shown necessary;\n- conclusions that must be discarded.\n\nFailure of the theorem does not erase valid algebra. Valid algebra does not rescue the failed theorem.\n# Adversarial Audit\n\nUse this reference before calling a result decisive, closing a loophole, declaring a route impossible, or claiming that only technical work remains.\n\n## Audit protocol\n\nFor every central claim or proposed next step, write:\n\n| Item | Question |\n|---|---|\n| Target claim | What exact statement is under attack? |\n| Strongest alternative | What is the most plausible competing explanation or obstruction? |\n| Failure signature | What observation or derivation would distinguish it? |\n| Cheapest discriminator | What is the smallest valid theory or experiment check? |\n| Validity gates | What would make the check numerically or logically uninterpretable? |\n| Consequence | Which claim-ladder rung changes under each outcome? |\n\nAttack the strongest viable alternative. Do not use a weak baseline merely because it is easy to defeat.\n\n## Structural attacks\n\n### Hidden information and memory\n\n- Check whether coefficients, initialization, or forcing use the future target trajectory.\n- Check whether a finite coordinate hides an arbitrary function, history, matrix, dataset, or unbounded-precision code.\n- Check whether the current state determines future evolution or whether untracked response/history variables survive.\n- Test restartability from an intermediate state when autonomy is claimed.\n\n### Nonclosure and tail escape\n\n- Identify the exact residual generated by truncation.\n- Check whether bounded norm implies compactness in the required topology; usually it does not.\n- Check whether high modes can feed low observables through multiplication, contraction, transpose, aliasing, or nonlinear interaction.\n- Distinguish small individual coefficients from a small collective tail.\n- Ask for a reachable-state estimate rather than a bound on an arbitrary ambient ball.\n\n### Error production versus propagation\n\n- Locate where omitted error is produced.\n- Locate the stability or propagator estimate that transports it.\n- Reject arguments that provide only stability but no small source term.\n- Check for nonnormal or transient amplification even when eigenvalues appear stable.\n\n### Limits and topology\n\n- Write the limit order explicitly.\n- Check every interchange of limit, expectation, derivative, integral, optimization, or long-time operation.\n- Verify tightness, uniform integrability, regularity, or compactness assumptions needed for the interchange.\n- Confirm that convergence in the chosen topology controls the named observables.\n- Separate compact-time results from all-time claims.\n\n### Witness versus existence\n\n- Determine whether a failure concerns the chosen basis, closure, solver, or state.\n- Check whether a modest admissible enrichment could repair the witness.\n- Do not use failure of one construction as a no-go theorem for the full approximation class.\n\n## Mundane-explanation attacks\n\nConstruct the strongest scientifically plausible nuisance explanation, such as:\n\n- effective linearity after matching gain, scale, spectrum, or another nuisance parameter;\n- frozen-feature, lazy, low-rank, or small-motion behavior;\n- interpolation rather than autonomous prediction;\n- observable insensitivity despite wrong internal dynamics;\n- a special symmetry or data degeneracy;\n- an easier regime introduced by the testbed.\n\nUse a matched control that grants the nuisance explanation its best legitimate parameters without giving it the target trajectory. Add a mechanism-specific ablation when possible.\n\nClosing a loophole requires evidence against this strongest version, not against identity dynamics or another straw man.\n\n## Numerical and empirical attacks\n\n- Check conditioning before interpreting high-order behavior.\n- Use a common reference when comparing successive approximations.\n- Separate discretization, quadrature, sampling, optimization, and model errors.\n- Replicate any conclusion that is comparable to numerical variability.\n- Freeze primary metrics and thresholds before inspecting the decisive result.\n- Track negative and failed runs, not only favorable configurations.\n- Distinguish a theory-selected stress test from a searched favorable example.\n- Check source hashes, configurations, seeds, and raw-result provenance.\n- Treat failure of a validity gate as inconclusive, not as evidence for either hypothesis.\n\n## Logical severity\n\nClassify each surviving issue:\n\n- **Fatal:** contradicts the central conjecture under its stated contract.\n- **Witness-fatal:** defeats the current construction but not the existence claim.\n- **Major:** blocks a necessary bridge between claim-ladder rungs.\n- **Conditional:** matters only if a named assumption fails.\n- **Minor:** affects constants, presentation, or a noncentral extension.\n\nDo not call a gap \u201ctechnical\u201d unless a known theorem or fully specified argument appears to close it under already verified hypotheses.\n\n## Evidence language\n\nUse these distinctions:\n\n- **Ruled out:** logically contradicted or rejected by a valid, sufficiently powered discriminator over the stated scope.\n- **Strongly disfavored:** survives logically but conflicts with replicated evidence.\n- **Disfavored:** has contrary evidence with meaningful caveats.\n- **Unsupported:** lacks positive evidence.\n- **Open:** viable evidence exists on both sides or the decisive check is missing.\n- **Inconclusive:** the attempted check failed a logical or numerical validity gate.\n\n\u201cNo failure observed\u201d is not \u201cruled out.\u201d \u201cAccurate on tested observables\u201d is not \u201cmechanism identified.\u201d\n\n## Final hostile questions\n\nBefore upgrading the research claim, answer:\n\n1. What is the strongest surviving ordinary explanation?\n2. What is the strongest structural obstruction?\n3. What numerical artifact could mimic the result?\n4. Which necessary claim-ladder bridge remains assumed?\n5. Would the opposite result have changed the conclusion?\n6. Was that decision rule fixed before observing the result?\n7. Does the evidence generalize beyond the selected instances?\n8. What would still make the central claim false?\n# Bounded Multi-Route Proof Search\n\nUse this mode to search for a complete proof of a mathematical conjecture across several genuinely different approaches. Do not treat the mode, the user's optimism, or the conjecture's fame as evidence that a proof exists.\n\n## Fix the proof contract\n\nBefore choosing approaches, record:\n\n- the exact theorem, quantifiers, hypotheses, definitions, and edge cases;\n- which classical or supplied results may be used;\n- what counts as a complete proof;\n- which special cases, reductions, computations, or conditional results do not resolve the target;\n- the time, compute, token, and concurrency budget;\n- the terminal outcomes: a proof that survives audit, or budget exhaustion with exact proved results and gaps.\n\nDo not prohibit literature search unless the user requests an independent-discovery or benchmark-integrity mode. Do not assume an affirmative proof exists unless the user explicitly poses a hypothetical; even then, accept only a complete audited argument.\n\n## Choose the execution mode\n\nUse delegated or parallel workers only when the user explicitly authorizes delegation or parallel work and the environment supports it. Otherwise explore the routes sequentially with the same records and stopping rules. Never hard-code an agent count, runtime, or product-specific orchestration feature.\n\nAllocate available effort dynamically. Match the portfolio size to the budget, keep synthesis capacity in reserve, and reduce breadth when routes cannot be audited adequately.\n\n## Seed a diverse portfolio\n\nDefine approach families by mathematical mechanism, not wording. Choose only families appropriate to the theorem, such as structural induction, extremal arguments, algebraic or analytic reformulation, probabilistic construction, duality, invariants, decomposition, topology, optimization, or computation-assisted discovery.\n\nDuring the first round, preserve independence:\n\n- give each route the same proof contract without presenting a favored strategy as established;\n- avoid duplicating superficially different versions of one mechanism;\n- keep incompatible approaches alive long enough to expose their real strengths and obstructions;\n- reserve cross-pollination until routes have produced concrete mathematics.\n\n## Maintain an approach registry\n\nKeep one row per route:\n\n| Field | Record |\n|---|---|\n| Route | Stable identifier and approach family |\n| Mechanism | Proposed reason the route could prove the theorem |\n| Deliverable | Lemma, construction, equation, reduction, or counterexample sought |\n| Dependencies | External theorems and unproved internal claims |\n| Strongest result | Exact statement currently established |\n| Obstacle | Smallest unresolved step and its logical strength |\n| Distinctiveness | How the route differs from active alternatives |\n| Status | Active, promising, blocked, refuted, merged, or complete |\n| Reopen condition | New mechanism, lemma, or evidence required |\n\nUpdate the registry after every round. Cluster routes that use the same mathematical idea even when their notation differs.\n\n## Enforce a concrete route contract\n\nRequire every route to return:\n\n1. a precise claim or construction;\n2. a derivation or proof of everything asserted;\n3. every external dependency and its checked hypotheses;\n4. a counterexample search or sanity check when applicable;\n5. the exact unresolved implication, if any;\n6. a registry-status recommendation and reason.\n\nReject vague progress reports, unsupported optimism, and claims that a global compatibility step is routine. A reduction is meaningful progress only when its target is already proved, strictly better understood, or accompanied by a new mechanism that attacks it.\n\n## Run adaptive rounds\n\nAfter each round:\n\n1. verify concrete outputs before treating them as progress;\n2. merge duplicate routes by mechanism;\n3. identify reductions whose missing lemma is equivalent in strength to the original theorem;\n4. redirect effort toward underexplored families or newly enabled bottlenecks;\n5. mark a route blocked when it stalls at a theorem-strength lemma;\n6. reopen a blocked route only after a materially new invariant, construction, theorem, or counterexample appears;\n7. cross-pollinate mature routes when one route supplies another's missing ingredient;\n8. preserve valid lemmas and counterexamples from failed routes.\n\nDo not let elegance, early popularity, or the amount of work already invested determine allocation.\n\n## Audit candidate proofs independently\n\nWhen a complete route appears, stop expanding it and begin reconstruction. Assign separate checks, sequentially or through authorized workers, to:\n\n- reconstruct every implication from the proof contract;\n- verify all cited theorem statements and hypotheses;\n- test definitions, degenerate cases, boundary cases, and well-definedness;\n- search for circular dependence or an equivalent-strength hidden lemma;\n- generate the theorem-specific failure checklist;\n- seek a minimal counterexample to every vulnerable intermediate claim;\n- distinguish a complete proof from a plausible proof sketch.\n\nIntegrate the final mathematical argument using the rigorous proof workflow. Label it `candidate proof` until the full chain survives independent audit, and state whether any formal or machine verification was actually performed.\n\n## Stop and report honestly\n\nStop when a complete proof survives the authorized audit, or when the hard budget is exhausted. At exhaustion, report the registry, all proved lemmas, refuted and blocked routes, exact remaining gaps, and each route's reopen condition. Never convert persistence instructions into permission to fabricate a proof or conceal an unresolved implication.\n", "truncated": false}}

Source hierarchy\n\nTreat conversation recollection as a locator, not as the technical authority. Prefer:\n\n1. checked derivations, proofs, and raw experimental outputs;\n2. source code, frozen configurations, and provenance manifests;\n3. audited final reports tied to those artifacts;\n4. provisional notes and intermediate reports;\n5. conversational summaries.\n\nRecency alone does not establish correctness. A later statement supersedes an earlier one only when it uses corrected assumptions, better evidence, or a valid argument that changes the conclusion.\n\n## 2. Claim record\n\nMaintain one record per central claim:\n\n```markdown\n### C-<number>: <short name>\n\n- Statement:\n- Claim-ladder rung:\n- Status:\n- Scope and assumptions:\n- Supporting evidence:\n- Contrary evidence:\n- Dependencies:\n- Cheapest decisive resolver:\n- Concrete falsifier:\n- Supersedes:\n- Superseded by:\n- Authoritative sources:\n```\n\nKeep the statement narrow enough that one status applies.\n\n## 3. Status vocabulary\n\nUse one primary status:\n\n| Status | Meaning |\n|---|---|\n| Proved | Established by a complete argument under stated hypotheses |\n| Exact-under-assumptions | Algebraically or logically exact once named assumptions are granted |\n| Empirically-supported | Supported over a specified tested scope |\n| Strongly-disfavored | Conflicts with replicated valid evidence but is not logically impossible |\n| Disfavored | Has meaningful contrary evidence with caveats |\n| Falsified | Contradicted over the exact stated scope |\n| Open | A necessary bridge or discriminator is missing |\n| Inconclusive | The attempted test failed a validity gate or decision threshold |\n| Superseded | Replaced by a more valid formulation or result |\n\nAdd a confidence qualifier only when it clarifies evidence quality; do not substitute confidence for status.\n\n## 4. Supersession protocol\n\nWhen a result changes:\n\n1. Identify the exact old claim.\n2. Identify what changed: statement, assumption, method, data, numerical validity, or interpretation.\n3. Decide whether the old claim is falsified, narrowed, or merely superseded.\n4. Point the old record to the new one.\n5. Use only the new valid conclusion in the main narrative.\n6. Preserve the old record for provenance, not as a competing conclusion.\n\nExamples of legitimate supersession:\n\n- a parity or indexing correction invalidates an old comparison;\n- a well-conditioned replication overturns a numerically confounded trend;\n- a stronger matched control defeats the earlier baseline inference;\n- a proof audit finds a missing hypothesis;\n- a later theorem establishes a previously assumed bridge.\n\nA newer report that merely restates a claim does not supersede its source evidence.\n\n## 5. Contradiction audit\n\nFor apparently conflicting artifacts, compare:\n\n| Dimension | Questions |\n|---|---|\n| Target | Are they answering the same conjecture and observables? |\n| Scope | Are data class, horizon, and limit order identical? |\n| Approximation axis | Are they varying the same cutoff or resolution? |\n| Reference | Do they compare against the same ground truth? |\n| Numerics | Did either fail conditioning or replication gates? |\n| Method | Did one repair a known flaw in the other? |\n| Claim level | Is one empirical while the other asserts convergence? |\n\nOften the correct resolution is two scoped claims, not one winner.\n\n## 6. Causal update\n\nAfter each meaningful result, add:\n\n```markdown\n### Update U-<number>\n\n- New evidence:\n- Validity scope:\n- Mechanism affected:\n- Claims upgraded:\n- Claims downgraded:\n- Claims unchanged and why:\n- Superseded conclusion:\n- Newly exposed dependency:\n- Authorized next branch, if any:\n```\n\nState why unchanged claims do not follow from the result. This prevents a successful experiment from silently upgrading unrelated theorem obligations.\n\n## 7. Research-state summary\n\nMaintain a compact table:\n\n| Category | Contents |\n|---|---|\n| Established | Proofs, exact constructions, and verified identities |\n| Supported | Empirical or conditional claims with stated scope |\n| Disfavored | Alternative explanations with contrary evidence |\n| Falsified | Claims rejected over an exact scope |\n| Open | Necessary unresolved assumptions and proof obligations |\n| Superseded | Old conclusions replaced by corrected ones |\n\nRank open items by logical leverage rather than by ease.\n\n## 8. Master-report protocol\n\nWrite an authoritative report in this order:\n\n1. current bottom line;\n2. canonical contract and sharp conjecture;\n3. exact causal or mathematical skeleton;\n4. strongest theoretical support;\n5. empirical evidence and validity scope;\n6. adversarial alternatives and matched controls;\n7. established, supported, disfavored, falsified, and open claims;\n8. supersession note for materially changed conclusions;\n9. highest-leverage unresolved obligation.\n\nDo not narrate every historical iteration. Include history only when it explains why a conclusion changed or why a tempting argument is invalid.\n\n## 9. Failed-program salvage\n\nWhen an approach fails, inventory separately:\n\n- exact identities that remain valid;\n- useful representations or algorithms;\n- counterexamples and no-go results;\n- causal mechanisms exposed;\n- assumptions shown necessary;\n- conclusions that must be discarded.\n\nFailure of the theorem does not erase valid algebra. Valid algebra does not rescue the failed theorem.\n", "truncated": false}}

No canonical transfer is proved.\n\nUse [Panahi v1, PDF equations (6), (9)](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2603.09310v1),\nwith one mixture component, \\(R=I,J=1,z=0\\).\n\n**Exact frozen-history lemma.** Let \\(n,m,K\\) be positive integers,\n\\(\\eta,\\sigma>0\\), and \\(T=K\\eta\\). Fix deterministic columns\n\\(\\theta_l\\in\\mathbb R^n,\\omega_l\\in\\mathbb R^m\\) for\n\\(1\\le l\\le K\\), and let \\(\\Gamma\\in\\mathbb R^{K\\times K}\\)\nhave independent standard real Gaussian entries. Put\n\\(\\Theta=[\\theta_1,\\ldots,\\theta_K]\\),\n\\(\\Omega=[\\omega_1,\\ldots,\\omega_K]\\), and take upper Cholesky\nfactors with positive diagonal:\n\\[\nA_\\theta^TA_\\theta=\\Theta^T\\Theta+\\sigma^2I,\\qquad\nA_\\omega^TA_\\omega=\\Omega^T\\Omega/m+\\sigma^2I.\n\\]\nSet \\(t_i=(\\Theta A_\\theta^{-1})_i\\) and\n\\(s_i=(\\Omega A_\\omega^{-1})_i/\\sqrt m\\), for column index \\(i\\).\nThe two covariance-matching terms in (9) are exactly\n\\[\ng_l=\\frac1{\\sqrt m}\\sum_{i\\le l}t_i(\\Gamma A_\\omega)_{il},\\qquad\nk_l=\\frac1{\\sqrt m}\\sum_{i<l}s_i(\\Gamma^TA_\\theta)_{il},\n\\]\nwhere \\(k_l\\) is the perturbation of \\(p_l/\\sqrt m\\).\n\nThen the following identities and estimates hold. They evaluate the added\nforcing, not the difference between two adaptive solutions.\n\\[\n\\mathbb E\\left\\|\\eta\\sum_{l=1}^K g_l\\right\\|^2\n=\\frac{\\eta^2}{m}\\sum_{i=1}^K\\|t_i\\|^2\n\\left[\\frac1m\\left\\|\\sum_{l=i}^K\\omega_l\\right\\|^2\n+\\sigma^2(K-i+1)\\right],                                      \\tag{A}\n\\]\n\\[\n\\mathbb E\\left\\|\\eta\\sum_{l=1}^K k_l\\right\\|^2\n=\\frac{\\eta^2}{m}\\sum_{i=1}^{K-1}\\|s_i\\|^2\n\\left[\\left\\|\\sum_{l=i+1}^K\\theta_l\\right\\|^2\n+\\sigma^2(K-i)\\right].                                       \\tag{B}\n\\]\n**Proof of the identities.** Group each independent \\(\\Gamma_{ij}\\) in (A):\nits coefficient is \\(\\eta t_i\\sum_{l\\ge i}(A_\\omega)_{jl}/\\sqrt m\\).\nSumming over \\(j\\) produces the suffix quadratic form of\n\\(A_\\omega^TA_\\omega\\). For (B), the coefficient of\n\\(\\Gamma_{ji}\\) is\n\\(\\eta s_i\\sum_{l>i}(A_\\theta)_{jl}/\\sqrt m\\), giving the\nstrict suffix and (B). Cross terms between distinct Gaussian entries\nhave zero expectation. The strict inequality in (B) is essential.\n\nFor repeated queries \\(\\theta_l=\\theta,\\omega_l=\\omega\\), put\n\\(a=\\|\\theta\\|>0,b=\\|\\omega\\|/\\sqrt m>0\\). The regularized upper\nCholesky factor of \\(a^2\\mathbf1\\mathbf1^T+\\sigma^2I\\) satisfies\n\\[\n(A_a)_{ii}=\\sigma\\sqrt{\\frac{\\sigma^2+ia^2}{\\sigma^2+(i-1)a^2}},\n\\quad\n(A_a)_{ij}=\\frac{a^2\\sigma}\n {\\sqrt{(\\sigma^2+(i-1)a^2)(\\sigma^2+ia^2)}}\\quad(i<j).\n\\]\nConsequently \\(t_i=\\theta d_i(a)\\), where\n\\[\nd_i(a)=\\frac{\\sigma}\n {\\sqrt{(\\sigma^2+(i-1)a^2)(\\sigma^2+ia^2)}},\\qquad\n\\sum_{i=1}^l d_i(a)^2=\\frac{l}{\\sigma^2+la^2}.\n\\]\nSubstitution gives\n\\[\n\\mathbb E\\left\\|\\eta\\sum_lg_l\\right\\|^2\n=\\frac{a^2\\eta^2}{m}\\sum_{i=1}^Kd_i(a)^2\n [b^2(K-i+1)^2+\\sigma^2(K-i+1)]\n\\le\\frac{b^2T^2+\\sigma^2T\\eta}{m}.                            \\tag{C}\n\\]\nThe analogous bound for \\(\\eta\\sum_lk_l\\) is\n\\((a^2T^2+\\sigma^2T\\eta)/m\\), using \\(i<K\\) and \\(K-i\\).\nIn the fixed-\\(K\\), \\(\\sigma\\downarrow0\\) limit,\n\\(g_l\\to(\\theta/a)(b/\\sqrt m)\\Gamma_{11}\\) for every \\(l\\),\nwhereas \\(k_1=0\\) and\n\\(k_l\\to(\\omega/(b\\sqrt m))(a/\\sqrt m)\\Gamma_{11}\\) for \\(l\\ge2\\).\nThe forcing is coherent in time: Euler integration yields \\(T/\\sqrt m\\),\nnot a \\(\\sqrt K\\) growth. Inequality (C) is already uniform in both\n\\(K\\) and \\(\\sigma\\), so this conclusion does not exchange limits.\n\nMore generally, with \\(\\|\\omega_l\\|/\\sqrt m\\le B\\), (A) is bounded by\n\\[\n\\frac{T^2B^2+\\sigma^2T\\eta}{m}\\,\nr_{\\rm eff}(\\Theta,\\sigma),\\qquad\nr_{\\rm eff}=\\operatorname{tr}\\!\\left[\n\\Theta^T\\Theta(\\Theta^T\\Theta+\\sigma^2I)^{-1}\\right].           \\tag{D}\n\\]\nA deterministic finite-rank path has bounded effective rank, including\nan affine slowly changing history. This follows because\n\\(\\sum_i\\|t_i\\|^2=r_{\\rm eff}\\), by cyclicity of the trace and\n\\(A_\\theta^{-1}A_\\theta^{-T}=(\\Theta^T\\Theta+\\sigma^2I)^{-1}\\).\nThe reverse orientation has the analogous estimate with\n\\(r_{\\rm eff}(\\Omega/\\sqrt m,\\sigma)\\) and\n\\(A=\\max_l\\|\\theta_l\\|\\).\n\nThese estimates also give convergence in the supremum norm of the\nintegrated path, not merely convergence at its last time. Specifically,\n\\[\n\\mathbb E\\max_{1\\le j\\le K}\n \\left\\|\\eta\\sum_{l=1}^j g_l\\right\\|^2\n\\le \\frac{T^2(B^2+\\sigma^2)}m\\,r_{\\rm eff}(\\Theta,\\sigma),      \\tag{E}\n\\]\nand the reverse orientation has bound\n\\(T^2(A^2+\\sigma^2)r_{\\rm eff}(\\Omega/\\sqrt m,\\sigma)/m\\).\nIndeed, pathwise Cauchy\u2013Schwarz bounds the maximum by\n\\(T\\eta\\sum_l\\|g_l\\|^2\\), while Gaussian isometry gives\n\\(\\mathbb E\\|g_l\\|^2=(\\|\\omega_l\\|^2/m+\\sigma^2)\n \\sum_{i\\le l}\\|t_i\\|^2/m\\).\n\nThe unintegrated frozen queries also admit a uniform maximum bound:\n\\[\n\\mathbb E\\max_{1\\le l\\le K}\\|g_l\\|^2\n\\le\\frac{4(B^2+\\sigma^2)}m\\,\n r_{\\rm eff}(\\Theta,\\sigma)\\log(2K),                           \\tag{F}\n\\]\nwith the analogous reverse-orientation estimate. To verify this without\nany independence between times, let \\(Z_l\\) be centered Gaussian\nvectors with \\(\\mathbb E\\|Z_l\\|^2\\le v^2\\). For \\(v>0\\), the\ncovariance eigenvalues \\(\\lambda_j\\) are at most \\(v^2\\), and\n\\[\n\\log\\mathbb E e^{\\|Z_l\\|^2/(4v^2)}\n=-\\tfrac12\\sum_j\\log(1-\\lambda_j/(2v^2))\\le\\tfrac12.\n\\]\nHere \\(-\\log(1-x)\\le2x\\) for \\(0\\le x\\le1/2\\) was used.\nJensen's inequality and \\(\\max_l e^{x_l}\\le\\sum_l e^{x_l}\\)\ngive \\(\\mathbb E\\max_l\\|Z_l\\|^2\\le4v^2\\log(2K)\\).\nThe case \\(v=0\\) is immediate. Apply this with\n\\(v^2=(B^2+\\sigma^2)r_{\\rm eff}/m\\), using the per-query identity\nabove. Thus (F) also includes arbitrarily correlated frozen query times.\n\n**Slow-history bound, including integer choices.** Assume only the discrete\nLipschitz estimate\n\\(\\|\\theta_l-\\theta_j\\|\\le M\\eta|l-j|\\). Then\n\\[\nr_{\\rm eff}\\le r+\\frac{KM^2T^2}{\\sigma^2r^2}\\quad(1\\le r\\le K).\n\\]\nTo prove this, partition the indices into the nonempty blocks\n\\(\\lfloor(j-1)K/r\\rfloor<l\\le\\lfloor jK/r\\rfloor\\),\n\\(1\\le j\\le r\\), replacing each column by the first column of its\nblock. Each block spans at most \\(\\eta(\\lceil K/r\\rceil-1)\\le T/r\\)\nin physical time. This rank-at-most-\\(r\\) matrix approximation therefore\nhas squared Frobenius error at most \\(K(MT/r)^2\\). The sum of squared\nsingular values after the first \\(r\\) is no larger than that error;\nthe first \\(r\\) terms of the effective rank are at most one each, and\nthe rest are at most their squared singular values divided by\n\\(\\sigma^2\\). This proves the estimate.\n\nSet \\(x=(KM^2T^2/\\sigma^2)^{1/3}\\) and choose\n\\(r=\\min\\{K,\\max\\{1,\\lceil x\\rceil\\}\\}\\). If \\(x\\le K\\),\nthe estimate is at most \\(1+2x\\); if \\(x>K\\), use\n\\(r_{\\rm eff}\\le K\\) directly. Thus\n\\[\nr_{\\rm eff}\\le\\min\\{K,1+2(KM^2T^2/\\sigma^2)^{1/3}\\}.\n\\]\nFor \\(K=O(m^2)\\), uniformly bounded \\(M,T\\), and\n\\(\\sigma\\gg m^{-1/2}\\), this proves \\(r_{\\rm eff}/m\\to0\\).\nChoosing additionally \\(\\sigma\\to0\\), for example\n\\(\\sigma=m^{-\\beta}\\), \\(0<\\beta<1/2\\), removes the added\nregularization forcing below. The reverse orientation needs the same\nLipschitz control of \\(\\omega_l/\\sqrt m\\).\n\nThe additive \\(\\sigma U/\\sqrt m\\), \\(\\sigma V\\) terms in (9) have\nEuler-integrated mean-square norms \\(\\sigma^2(n/m)T\\eta\\) and\n\\(\\sigma^2T\\eta\\), respectively, in the same two normalized spaces.\nFor independent standard Gaussian \\(U,V\\), their partial sums are\nsquare-integrable vector martingales; the expected squared maximum is at\nmost four times these quantities by the \\(L^2\\) maximal inequality.\nThe same Gaussian maximum argument bounds the expected squared maximum\nof their unintegrated query values by\n\\(4\\sigma^2(n/m)\\log(2K)\\) and \\(4\\sigma^2\\log(2K)\\).\n\nThus, for deterministic histories with uniformly bounded\n\\(A,B,T,n/m\\) and both discrete Lipschitz constants,\n\\(K\\asymp m^2\\), and \\(\\sigma=m^{-1/4}\\), each expected squared\nsupremum of the integrated covariance perturbation is \\(O(m^{-1/6})\\).\nEach additive-noise bound is \\(O(m^{-5/2})\\). Both orientations of the\nfull frozen primitive consequently vanish in \\(L^2\\) of the supremum\nnorm. This is a consequence of (E), without a union bound or logarithmic\nloss; it is not a statement about the unfrozen dynamics.\nFurthermore, (F) gives expected squared raw-query maxima\n\\(O(m^{-1/6}\\log m)\\), and the additive terms contribute\n\\(O(m^{-1/2}\\log m)\\). Hence the unintegrated frozen perturbations\nalso vanish uniformly over the mesh.\n\nThe implication is scoped. Simple deterministic histories resolve the\nraw Gaussian-matrix-size concern, even with \\(K\\asymp m^2\\).\nHowever, in the actual comparison dynamics the Cholesky factors and\nqueries depend on \\(\\Gamma\\); conditioning on those histories does not\npreserve the independent Gaussian law used in (A)\u2013(B) or (F). Thus \\(X\\) has independent\nstandard Gaussian entries. All auxiliary Gaussian arrays are independent\nof \\(X\\) and each other, as in the source. Let \\(\\sigma>0\\),\n\\(K\\ge2\\), \\(\\eta>0\\), \\(T=K\\eta\\), and let \\(e_1\\) denote\nthe first coordinate vector. Define the queries by\n\\[\n\\theta_l=e_1,\\qquad \\omega_1=\\sqrt m\\,e_1,\\quad w_1=1,\\qquad\n\\omega_l=\\sqrt m\\,w_l e_1,\\quad\nw_l=1+\\tau_l\\tanh S,\\quad \\tau_l=(l-1)\\eta,\\quad l\\ge2,\n\\]\nwhere \\(S=\\sqrt m\\,(q_1)_1\\). This is a valid smooth causal query\nrule: \\(\\theta_l\\) ignores previous outputs, and \\(\\omega_l\\)\nuses only the already observed \\(q_1\\) when \\(l\\ge2\\). Every\n\\(p_l\\) is generated in the prescribed order but is ignored by these\nquery maps. Hence the order remains\n\\(\\theta_l\\to p_l\\to\\omega_l\\to q_l\\).\n\nThe first step of (9) gives exactly\n\\[\nq_1=\\frac{X e_1+\\sigma U_1+\\Gamma_{11}e_1}{\\sqrt m},\\qquad\nS=X_{11}+\\sigma(U_1)_1+\\Gamma_{11}\\sim N(0,v),\\quad v=2+\\sigma^2.\n\\]\nIndeed, both first Cholesky diagonals are\n\\(\\sqrt{1+\\sigma^2}\\), so their factors cancel in the first\n\\(\\Gamma\\)-term. The reverse \\(\\Gamma\\)-term at \\(p_1\\) is zero\nbecause its triangular part excludes the diagonal.\n\nBoth histories satisfy the proposed path premises, almost surely:\n\\[\n\\|\\theta_l\\|=1,\\quad\\|\\omega_l\\|/\\sqrt m\\le1+T,\\quad\n\\|\\omega_l-\\omega_j\\|/\\sqrt m\\le\\eta|l-j|.\n\\]\nBoth history matrices have rank one; each regularized effective rank is\nat most one. These are temporal bounds. No dimension-independent\nLipschitz bound of the query map as a function of arbitrary inputs is\nbeing asserted.\n\nWrite \\(s=\\sigma^2\\), and let\n\\[\nd_i=\\frac{\\sigma}{\\sqrt{(s+i-1)(s+i)}},\\qquad\n(\\Theta A_\\theta^{-1})_i=e_1d_i,\\qquad\n\\sum_{i=1}^l d_i^2=\\frac{l}{s+l}.\n\\]\nThe covariance perturbation of \\(q_l\\) is\n\\[\ng_l=\\frac{e_1}{\\sqrt m}\\sum_{i\\le l}d_i(\\Gamma A_\\omega)_{il}.\n\\]\nHere \\(A_\\omega\\) is a function of \\(S\\) alone. For \\(l\\ge2\\),\n\\((A_\\omega)_{1l}=w_l/\\sqrt{1+s}\\), so the coefficient of\n\\(\\Gamma_{11}\\) in \\(g_l\\) is\n\\(e_1w_l/[\\sqrt m(1+s)]\\). Every other entry of \\(\\Gamma\\)\nis independent of \\(S,\\Gamma_{11}\\) and remains centered. Therefore\nGaussian integration by parts gives\n\\[\n\\mathbb E g_l=\n\\frac{e_1\\tau_l}{\\sqrt m(1+s)}\\,\n\\mathbb E\\operatorname{sech}^2S\\ne0\\quad(l\\ge2).              \\tag{1}\n\\]\n\nThere is also a strict failure of the frozen second-moment formula. Put\n\\[\nF_l=\\frac1m\\mathbb E\\left[(w_l^2+s)\\sum_{i\\le l}d_i^2\\right],\n\\qquad\nC_s=\\mathbb E[(\\Gamma_{11}^2-1)\\tanh^2S].\n\\]\nThe expression \\(F_l\\) is what the deterministic-history isometry would\ngive if inserted under expectation without an adaptation correction.\nConditioning on \\(S,\\Gamma_{11}\\), all cross terms with the other\nGaussian entries vanish, yielding exactly\n\\[\n\\mathbb E\\|g_l\\|^2-F_l\n=\\frac{\\mathbb E[(\\Gamma_{11}^2-1)w_l^2]}{m(1+s)^2}\n=\\frac{\\tau_l^2 C_s}{m(1+s)^2}>0\\quad(l\\ge2).                 \\tag{2}\n\\]\nThe constant and linear terms in \\(w_l^2\\) vanish respectively by\ncentering and the simultaneous sign symmetry of\n\\((S,\\Gamma_{11})\\). Strict positivity follows from\n\\[\n\\mathbb E[\\Gamma_{11}^2-1\\mid S]=\\frac{S^2-v}{v^2},\\qquad\nC_s=\\frac{\\operatorname{Cov}(S^2,\\tanh^2S)}{v^2}>0.\n\\]\nTo check the last sign, \\(x\\mapsto\\tanh^2\\sqrt x\\) is strictly\nincreasing on \\([0,\\infty)\\), and for an independent copy \\(Y'\\)\nof the nondegenerate variable \\(Y=S^2\\), twice this covariance is\n\\(\\mathbb E[(Y-Y')(f(Y)-f(Y'))]>0\\).\n\nThe integrated isometry fails in the same explicit way. For\n\\(P_K=\\eta\\sum_{l=1}^K g_l\\), let\n\\[\n\\mathcal F_K=\\frac{\\eta^2}{m}\\mathbb E\n \\sum_{i=1}^K d_i^2\n \\left[\\left(\\sum_{l=i}^K w_l\\right)^2+s(K-i+1)\\right],\\qquad\nH=\\eta\\sum_{l=2}^K\\tau_l=\\frac{\\eta^2K(K-1)}2.\n\\]\nThis is the frozen suffix formula evaluated on the adaptive history.\nBecause \\(g_1=e_1\\Gamma_{11}/\\sqrt m\\), the coefficient of\n\\(\\Gamma_{11}\\) in \\(P_K\\) is\n\\(e_1[T+\\eta s+H\\tanh S]/[\\sqrt m(1+s)]\\). The preceding\nconditioning and symmetry argument therefore gives\n\\[\n\\mathbb E P_K=\\frac{e_1H}{\\sqrt m(1+s)}\n \\mathbb E\\operatorname{sech}^2S,\\qquad\n\\mathbb E\\|P_K\\|^2-\\mathcal F_K\n=\\frac{H^2C_s}{m(1+s)^2}>0.                                   \\tag{3}\n\\]\nFor \\(K=2\\), \\(H=\\eta^2\\), recovering the two-step discrepancy\n\\(\\eta^4C_s/[m(1+s)^2]\\). For fixed physical \\(T\\) and\n\\(\\eta\\downarrow0\\), \\(H\\to T^2/2\\): small temporal increments\ndo not remove this correction relative to the natural \\(1/m\\)\nsecond-moment scale. Nevertheless the displayed correction itself is\n\\(O(1/m)\\), and is fully compatible with a vanishing-width bound.\n\nThe useful conclusion is precise: causality, bounded norms, temporal\nLipschitz control, and both Gram ranks being one do not preserve the\nfrozen Gaussian isometry or conditional centering. A prospective adaptive\nbound must estimate the retained covariance with the query-dependent\nCholesky coefficients, or supply a different self-normalized argument.\nThis example does not refute such a bound. It establishes no failure of\ncanonical trajectories, nonlinear stability, or unique restart.\nNo experiments were performed.\n", "truncated": false}}

With probability tending\nto one,\n\\[\n\\frac1p U_{[p]}U_{[p]}^\\top\\succeq\\frac12 I_m.                 \\tag{1}\n\\]\nHere \\(A\\succeq B\\) means \\(v^\\top(A-B)v\\ge0\\) for every \\(v\\).\nTo prove (1), take a \\(1/4\\)-net of the unit sphere with at most\n\\(9^m\\) points. For a centered Gaussian vector of covariance\n\\(C\\), independence in an orthonormal eigenbasis gives\n\\(\\operatorname{Var}(\\|Z\\|_2^2)=2\\operatorname{tr}(C^2)\\).\nConsequently the conditional variance here is at most\n\\(2(B^2+\\sigma^2)^2/m\\). By (2), its largest covariance\neigenvalue is at most \\((T^2B^2+\\sigma^2T\\eta)/m\\). The same\nconditional variance calculation proves the asserted convergence of\n\\(\\|P_K\\|_2^2\\).\n\n## Scope\n\nThe history is uniformly close to zero but is not uniformly\ntime-Lipschitz on this fine mesh. The smooth-history theorem therefore\nremains valid. This example rules out extending its conclusion merely\nby saying that the history has an additional \\(O(\\sigma)\\) jitter that\nvanishes uniformly. Regularized effective rank is sensitive to that\njitter when the number of queries greatly exceeds the width.\n\nThis also explains why an adaptive effective-rank bound, even if\nproved, would not automatically settle the network comparison.\nIts rank or temporal premise would have to be verified for the actual\nperturbed history, which receives fresh additive query noise.\nThe present construction is a frozen algebraic test, not a proof that\nthe canonical comparison has macroscopic perturbations, and not a\ncounterexample to the requested MF/GF limit. No experiments were used.\n", "truncated": false}}]}

TOOL {"type": "commandExecution", "id": "exec-da920c24-087e-4855-9fef-3748dc36b008", "command": "/bin/bash -lc 'cat /tmp/l3-full-resolution-9nbD4z/PANAHI_ADAPTIVE_GRAM_MARTINGALE.md'", "cwd": "/home/amir/Codes/PDE", "status": "completed", "exitCode": 0, "durationMs": 0, "output": {"text": "# Adaptive Gram martingale for the real comparison perturbation\n\nThis note proves a conditional adaptive estimate for the perturbations in\n[Panahi v1, PDF (6), (9)](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2603.09310v1), with one\ncentered mixture component, \\(R=I,J=1,z=0\\). It does not assume the\nqueries are independent of the auxiliary Gaussian matrix. The effective\nrank premise below remains a premise for the actual perturbed dynamics.\n\n## 1. Causal setup and exact identities\n\nFix \\(n,m,K\\ge1\\) and \\(\\sigma>0\\). Let \\(\\Gamma\\in\n\\mathbb R^{K\\times K}\\) have independent standard Gaussian entries.\nLet \\(\\mathcal H\\) contain all other random arrays and seeds, jointly\nindependent of \\(\\Gamma\\). In the perturbed-original process these\ninclude \\(X,U,V\\); revealing their entire arrays initially does not\nreveal any entry of \\(\\Gamma\\).\n\nUse finite-valued measurable causal query maps with the source's order\n\\(\\theta_l\\to p_l\\to\\omega_l\\to q_l\\). Define\n\\[\n\\mathcal F_l=\\mathcal H\\vee\\sigma(\\Gamma_{ij}:i,j\\le l),\\qquad\n\\mathcal F_{l-1/2}=\\mathcal F_{l-1}\\vee\n \\sigma(\\Gamma_{lj}:j<l).\n\\]\nHere \\(\\theta_l\\in\\mathbb R^n\\) is \\(\\mathcal F_{l-1}\\)-measurable,\n\\(p_l,\\omega_l\\in\\mathbb R^m\\) are\n\\(\\mathcal F_{l-1/2}\\)-measurable, and \\(q_l\\) is\n\\(\\mathcal F_l\\)-measurable. The assertions follow inductively from\n(9): its reverse strict-triangular term first uses the fresh row\n\\(\\Gamma_{lj},j<l\\); its forward triangular term subsequently uses\nthe fresh column \\(\\Gamma_{il},i\\le l\\). The other terms are already\nmeasurable from the queries and \\(\\mathcal H\\).\n\nFor each leading history take the positive-diagonal upper Cholesky\nfactors\n\\[\nA_\\theta^TA_\\theta=\\Theta^T\\Theta+\\sigma^2I,\\qquad\nA_\\omega^TA_\\omega=\\Omega^T\\Omega/m+\\sigma^2I.\n\\]\nThe leading blocks of these factors and their inverses agree between\nprefixes. Thus the columns\n\\[\nt_i=(\\Theta A_\\theta^{-1})_i\\in\\mathbb R^n,\\qquad\ns_i=(\\Omega A_\\omega^{-1})_i/\\sqrt m\\in\\mathbb R^m\n\\]\nnever change when later queries arrive. In particular \\(t_l\\) is\nknown before the fresh row and \\(s_l\\) before the fresh column.\nAn arbitrary horizon-dependent Cholesky sign convention is not allowed.\n\nPut\n\\[\n\\mathsf T_l=\\sum_{i\\le l}t_it_i^T\\preceq I_n,\\qquad\n\\mathsf S_l=\\sum_{i\\le l}s_is_i^T\\preceq I_m,\n\\quad \\rho_{\\theta,l}=\\operatorname{tr}\\mathsf T_l,\n\\quad \\rho_{\\omega,l}=\\operatorname{tr}\\mathsf S_l.              \\tag{1}\n\\]\nIndeed \\(\\mathsf T_l=\\Theta_l(\\Theta_l^T\\Theta_l+\n\\sigma^2I)^{-1}\\Theta_l^T\\), and the singular-value decomposition\ngives eigenvalues \\(d_j^2/(d_j^2+\\sigma^2)\\le1\\); the same\ncalculation applies to \\(\\Omega_l/\\sqrt m\\). These traces are the\nregularized effective ranks. They increase with the prefix, and\n\\(\\|t_i\\|,\\|s_i\\|\\le1\\).\n\nDefine the rectangular matrices\n\\[\nB_{l-1/2}=\\sum_{i\\le l,j<l}t_i\\Gamma_{ij}s_j^T,\\qquad\nB_l=\\sum_{i,j\\le l}t_i\\Gamma_{ij}s_j^T,\\qquad B_0=0.\n\\]\nTheir half-step increments are\n\\[\nB_{l-1/2}-B_{l-1}=t_l a_l^T,\n\\quad a_l=\\sum_{j<l}s_j\\Gamma_{lj},\\qquad\nB_l-B_{l-1/2}=b_l s_l^T,\n\\quad b_l=\\sum_{i\\le l}t_i\\Gamma_{il}.                        \\tag{2}\n\\]\nThe first fresh vector is conditionally \\(N(0,\\mathsf S_{l-1})\\),\nand the second is conditionally \\(N(0,\\mathsf T_l)\\), at their\nrespective preceding half-steps. Hence \\(B\\) is a rectangular matrix\nmartingale. Its increments have finite moments at every fixed finite\n\\(n,m,K\\), by (1) and the Gaussian moments. No bound on the raw\nquery norms is needed for this assertion.\n\nLet \\(g_l\\) be the forward \\(\\Gamma\\)-perturbation in (9), and\n\\(k_l\\) the reverse perturbation divided by \\(\\sqrt m\\).\nThe exact decompositions are\n\\[\ng_l=\\frac{B_l\\omega_l}{m}\n +\\frac{\\sigma^2}{\\sqrt m\\,A_{\\omega,ll}}b_l,\\qquad\nk_l=\\frac{B_{l-1/2}^T\\theta_l}{\\sqrt m}\n +\\frac{\\sigma^2}{\\sqrt m\\,A_{\\theta,ll}}a_l.                  \\tag{3}\n\\]\nFor \\(j\\le l\\), the Gram identity gives\n\\[\n\\frac{s_j^T\\omega_l}{\\sqrt m}\n=\\big[A_\\omega-\\sigma^2A_\\omega^{-T}\\big]_{jl},\n\\]\nso \\(A_{\\omega,jl}=s_j^T\\omega_l/\\sqrt m\\) for \\(j<l\\),\nand the diagonal has the additional \\(\\sigma^2/A_{\\omega,ll}\\).\nThe same identity holds for \\(A_\\theta\\). Substitution into the two\ntriangular terms of (9) proves (3), including the strict reverse sum.\nFurthermore, each diagonal obeys \\(A_{ll}\\ge\\sigma\\): its squared\nSchur complement is\n\\(\\sigma^2+x_l^T[I-X_{l-1}(X_{l-1}^TX_{l-1}+\n\\sigma^2I)^{-1}X_{l-1}^T]x_l\\ge\\sigma^2\\).\nThus both residual coefficients in (3), apart from \\(m^{-1/2}\\),\nare at most \\(\\sigma\\).\n\n## 2. Quantitative adaptive bound\n\nTake deterministic \\(R_\\theta,R_\\omega\\ge0\\),\n\\(r=\\max(R_\\theta,R_\\omega)>0\\), and \\(0<\\alpha<1\\). Set\n\\[\nE_R=\\{\\rho_{\\theta,K}\\le R_\\theta,\n       \\rho_{\\omega,K}\\le R_\\omega\\},\\quad\nu=\\log(4K/\\alpha),\\quad c=\\sqrt{2r+4u},\n\\quad v=\\log(2(n+m)/\\alpha),\n\\quad L=2\\sqrt{rv}+\\tfrac23cv.                               \\tag{4}\n\\]\nThen\n\\[\n\\mathbb P\\left(E_R\\cap\n \\left\\{\\max_h\\|B_h\\|_{\\rm op}>L\n \\ \\text{or}\\ \\max_l(\\|a_l\\|\\vee\\|b_l\\|)>c\\right\\}\\right)\n\\le\\alpha,                                                  \\tag{5}\n\\]\nwhere \\(h\\) runs over all half and full steps through \\(K\\).\nIf additionally\n\\(\\max_l\\|\\theta_l\\|\\le A\\),\n\\(\\max_l\\|\\omega_l\\|/\\sqrt m\\le C\\), then outside the same\nexceptional event, on \\(E_R\\),\n\\[\n\\max_l\\|g_l\\|\\le\\frac{CL+\\sigma c}{\\sqrt m},\\qquad\n\\max_l\\|k_l\\|\\le\\frac{AL+\\sigma c}{\\sqrt m}.                 \\tag{6}\n\\]\nThese norm bounds may instead be imposed on an event and intersected\nwith \\(E_R\\). If \\(r=0\\), both Gram contractions are zero on\n\\(E_R\\), and the claimed perturbations are zero there.\n\n### Proof, including localization and truncation\n\nFirst stop accepting increments permanently before a rank threshold\nwould be exceeded. Before row \\(l\\), both \\(\\rho_{\\theta,l}\\)\nand \\(\\rho_{\\omega,l-1}\\) are known; accept the row only while they\nare within their thresholds. Before column \\(l\\),\n\\(\\rho_{\\omega,l}\\) is also known; accept the column only if its\nthreshold has not been exceeded. These acceptance indicators are\npredictable at their respective half-steps. The accepted process agrees\nwith \\(B\\) on \\(E_R\\). Its coefficients still refer to the original\nadaptive histories; this construction does not alter the query dynamics.\n\nFor an accepted untruncated row increment \\(X=t_la_l^T\\), its two\nconditional second-moment matrices are\n\\[\n\\mathbb E[XX^T\\mid\\mathcal F_{l-1}]\n =\\rho_{\\omega,l-1}t_lt_l^T,\\qquad\n\\mathbb E[X^TX\\mid\\mathcal F_{l-1}]\n =\\|t_l\\|^2\\mathsf S_{l-1}.\n\\]\nFor an accepted column increment \\(Y=b_ls_l^T\\), they are\n\\[\n\\mathbb E[YY^T\\mid\\mathcal F_{l-1/2}]\n =\\|s_l\\|^2\\mathsf T_l,\\qquad\n\\mathbb E[Y^TY\\mid\\mathcal F_{l-1/2}]\n =\\rho_{\\theta,l}s_ls_l^T.\n\\]\nSumming along each realized path, (1) and the threshold rule imply\n\\[\nW_{\\rm left}\\preceq\n R_\\omega\\sum_{\\rm accepted\\ rows}t_lt_l^T\n +\\sum_{\\rm accepted\\ columns}\\|s_l\\|^2I_n\n \\preceq2R_\\omega I_n,\n\\]\n\\[\nW_{\\rm right}\\preceq\n \\sum_{\\rm accepted\\ rows}\\|t_l\\|^2I_m\n +R_\\theta\\sum_{\\rm accepted\\ columns}s_ls_l^T\n \\preceq2R_\\theta I_m.                                      \\tag{7}\n\\]\nIn particular both predictable quadratic variations have norm at most\n\\(2r\\). The unequal left/right constants matter: no factor \\(K\\)\nhas been introduced.\n\nTo bound increments without an unjustified Gaussian boundedness\nassumption, multiply each accepted row increment by\n\\(\\mathbf1_{\\{\\|a_l\\|\\le c\\}}\\), and each accepted column\nincrement by \\(\\mathbf1_{\\{\\|b_l\\|\\le c\\}}\\). Conditional\nGaussian symmetry makes the truncated increments centered. Their\nconditional second moments are bounded in positive-semidefinite order\nby the untruncated ones, so (7) remains valid. Their operator norms are\nat most \\(c\\), since \\(\\|t_l\\|,\\|s_l\\|\\le1\\).\n\nAt every accepted half-step the fresh Gaussian vector \\(Z\\) has\ncovariance at most the identity and trace at most \\(r\\). Its covariance\neigenvalues \\(\\lambda_j\\in[0,1]\\) give\n\\[\n\\mathbb E[e^{\\|Z\\|^2/4}\\mid\\text{past}]\n=\\prod_j(1-\\lambda_j/2)^{-1/2}\\le e^{r/2},\\qquad\n\\mathbb P(\\|Z\\|>c\\mid\\text{past})\\le e^{-u}.\n\\]\nThe product estimate uses \\(-\\log(1-x)\\le2x\\) for \\(x\\le1/2\\).\nA union bound over at most \\(2K\\) accepted half-steps costs at most\n\\(2Ke^{-u}=\\alpha/2\\). On its complement and \\(E_R\\), no\ntruncation or stopping occurred, and the truncated martingale agrees\nwith \\(B\\) at every half-step; all residual vectors have norm at most\n\\(c\\).\n\nApply [Tropp, *Freedman's inequality for matrix martingales*,\nCorollary 1.3](https://tropp.caltech.edu/papers/Tro11-Freedmans-Inequality.pdf).\nFor an \\(n\\times m\\) matrix martingale with increment norms at most\n\\(c\\) and both predictable quadratic variations at most \\(2r\\),\nthat corollary bounds the probability its norm ever exceeds \\(x\\) by\n\\[\n(n+m)\\exp\\left[-\\frac{x^2}{2(2r+cx/3)}\\right].\n\\]\nThe truncated process starts at zero, is adapted and centered, has bounded\nincrements and thus is integrable, and satisfies (7); these verify every\nhypothesis. For \\(x=L\\),\n\\(L^2\\ge2v(2r+cL/3)\\), so the bound is at most \\(\\alpha/2\\).\nCombining it with the truncation failure probability proves (5).\nFinally (3), \\(A_{ll}\\ge\\sigma\\), and the query norm bounds prove\n(6). None of these steps conditions on adaptive histories as though\ntheir Gaussian law were unchanged.\n\n## 3. What this resolves and what it does not\n\nFor \\(n\\asymp m\\), polynomial \\(K\\) and \\(\\alpha^{-1}\\),\n\\[\nL=O\\!\\left(\\sqrt{r\\log m}\n +\\sqrt{r+\\log m}\\,\\log m\\right).\n\\]\nWith bounded \\(A,C,\\sigma\\), the two adaptive \\(\\Gamma\\)-queries\nin (6) vanish uniformly whenever\n\\(r\\log^2m=o(m)\\), on rank events whose probability tends to one.\nFor an Euler primitive over a fixed physical horizon \\(T\\), its\nsupremum is at most \\(T\\) times the corresponding query supremum.\nThis conclusion is compatible with the earlier causal counterexample:\n\\(B\\) is a martingale, but multiplying it by adaptive queries need\nnot preserve centering or the frozen second-moment identity.\n\nThe additional \\(\\sigma U/\\sqrt m\\) and \\(\\sigma V/\\sqrt m\\)\nnormalized query noises have, by the same Gaussian norm calculation and\na union bound, maxima at most\n\\(\\sigma\\sqrt{2n/m+4\\log(2K/\\delta)/m}\\) and\n\\(\\sigma\\sqrt{2+4\\log(2K/\\delta)/m}\\), respectively, except on\nan event of probability at most \\(\\delta\\). They vanish when\n\\(n/m\\) stays bounded, \\(K\\) is polynomial, and \\(\\sigma\\to0\\).\nThis assertion concerns their size; it gives no effective-rank estimate\nfor the histories they generate.\n\nIn particular, the deterministic slow-history effective-rank estimate\ncannot simply be imposed on actual perturbed paths. Small direct query\nnoise can produce fresh directions at the same scale as the Gram\nregularization and invalidate the required rank bound. An actual rank\nestimate (or another replacement for that premise), nonlinear stability\nunder these query perturbations, regularization removal, and autonomous\nunique restart are still unproved. This note establishes no canonical\nnetwork convergence theorem. No experiments were performed.\n", "truncated": false}}

A later statement supersedes an earlier one only when it uses corrected assumptions, better evidence, or a valid argument that changes the conclusion.\n\n## 2. Claim record\n\nMaintain one record per central claim:\n\n```markdown\n### C-<number>: <short name>\n\n- Statement:\n- Claim-ladder rung:\n- Status:\n- Scope and assumptions:\n- Supporting evidence:\n- Contrary evidence:\n- Dependencies:\n- Cheapest decisive resolver:\n- Concrete falsifier:\n- Supersedes:\n- Superseded by:\n- Authoritative sources:\n```\n\nKeep the statement narrow enough that one status applies.\n\n## 3. Status vocabulary\n\nUse one primary status:\n\n| Status | Meaning |\n|---|---|\n| Proved | Established by a complete argument under stated hypotheses |\n| Exact-under-assumptions | Algebraically or logically exact once named assumptions are granted |\n| Empirically-supported | Supported over a specified tested scope |\n| Strongly-disfavored | Conflicts with replicated valid evidence but is not logically impossible |\n| Disfavored | Has meaningful contrary evidence with caveats |\n| Falsified | Contradicted over the exact stated scope |\n| Open | A necessary bridge or discriminator is missing |\n| Inconclusive | The attempted test failed a validity gate or decision threshold |\n| Superseded | Replaced by a more valid formulation or result |\n\nAdd a confidence qualifier only when it clarifies evidence quality; do not substitute confidence for status.\n\n## 4. Supersession protocol\n\nWhen a result changes:\n\n1. Identify the exact old claim.\n2. Identify what changed: statement, assumption, method, data, numerical validity, or interpretation.\n3. Decide whether the old claim is falsified, narrowed, or merely superseded.\n4. Point the old record to the new one.\n5. Use only the new valid conclusion in the main narrative.\n6. Preserve the old record for provenance, not as a competing conclusion.\n\nExamples of legitimate supersession:\n\n- a parity or indexing correction invalidates an old comparison;\n- a well-conditioned replication overturns a numerically confounded trend;\n- a stronger matched control defeats the earlier baseline inference;\n- a proof audit finds a missing hypothesis;\n- a later theorem establishes a previously assumed bridge.\n\nA newer report that merely restates a claim does not supersede its source evidence.\n\n## 5. Contradiction audit\n\nFor apparently conflicting artifacts, compare:\n\n| Dimension | Questions |\n|---|---|\n| Target | Are they answering the same conjecture and observables? |\n| Scope | Are data class, horizon, and limit order identical? |\n| Approximation axis | Are they varying the same cutoff or resolution? |\n| Reference | Do they compare against the same ground truth? |\n| Numerics | Did either fail conditioning or replication gates? |\n| Method | Did one repair a known flaw in the other? |\n| Claim level | Is one empirical while the other asserts convergence? |\n\nOften the correct resolution is two scoped claims, not one winner.\n\n## 6. Causal update\n\nAfter each meaningful result, add:\n\n```markdown\n### Update U-<number>\n\n- New evidence:\n- Validity scope:\n- Mechanism affected:\n- Claims upgraded:\n- Claims downgraded:\n- Claims unchanged and why:\n- Superseded conclusion:\n- Newly exposed dependency:\n- Authorized next branch, if any:\n```\n\nState why unchanged claims do not follow from the result. This prevents a successful experiment from silently upgrading unrelated theorem obligations.\n\n## 7. Research-state summary\n\nMaintain a compact table:\n\n| Category | Contents |\n|---|---|\n| Established | Proofs, exact constructions, and verified identities |\n| Supported | Empirical or conditional claims with stated scope |\n| Disfavored | Alternative explanations with contrary evidence |\n| Falsified | Claims rejected over an exact scope |\n| Open | Necessary unresolved assumptions and proof obligations |\n| Superseded | Old conclusions replaced by corrected ones |\n\nRank open items by logical leverage rather than by ease.\n\n## 8. Master-report protocol\n\nWrite an authoritative report in this order:\n\n1. current bottom line;\n2. canonical contract and sharp conjecture;\n3. exact causal or mathematical skeleton;\n4. strongest theoretical support;\n5. empirical evidence and validity scope;\n6. adversarial alternatives and matched controls;\n7. established, supported, disfavored, falsified, and open claims;\n8. supersession note for materially changed conclusions;\n9. highest-leverage unresolved obligation.\n\nDo not narrate every historical iteration. Include history only when it explains why a conclusion changed or why a tempting argument is invalid.\n\n## 9. Failed-program salvage\n\nWhen an approach fails, inventory separately:\n\n- exact identities that remain valid;\n- useful representations or algorithms;\n- counterexamples and no-go results;\n- causal mechanisms exposed;\n- assumptions shown necessary;\n- conclusions that must be discarded.\n\nFailure of the theorem does not erase valid algebra. Valid algebra does not rescue the failed theorem.\n", "truncated": false}}

Do not use a weak baseline merely because it is easy to defeat.\n\n## Structural attacks\n\n### Hidden information and memory\n\n- Check whether coefficients, initialization, or forcing use the future target trajectory.\n- Check whether a finite coordinate hides an arbitrary function, history, matrix, dataset, or unbounded-precision code.\n- Check whether the current state determines future evolution or whether untracked response/history variables survive.\n- Test restartability from an intermediate state when autonomy is claimed.\n\n### Nonclosure and tail escape\n\n- Identify the exact residual generated by truncation.\n- Check whether bounded norm implies compactness in the required topology; usually it does not.\n- Check whether high modes can feed low observables through multiplication, contraction, transpose, aliasing, or nonlinear interaction.\n- Distinguish small individual coefficients from a small collective tail.\n- Ask for a reachable-state estimate rather than a bound on an arbitrary ambient ball.\n\n### Error production versus propagation\n\n- Locate where omitted error is produced.\n- Locate the stability or propagator estimate that transports it.\n- Reject arguments that provide only stability but no small source term.\n- Check for nonnormal or transient amplification even when eigenvalues appear stable.\n\n### Limits and topology\n\n- Write the limit order explicitly.\n- Check every interchange of limit, expectation, derivative, integral, optimization, or long-time operation.\n- Verify tightness, uniform integrability, regularity, or compactness assumptions needed for the interchange.\n- Confirm that convergence in the chosen topology controls the named observables.\n- Separate compact-time results from all-time claims.\n\n### Witness versus existence\n\n- Determine whether a failure concerns the chosen basis, closure, solver, or state.\n- Check whether a modest admissible enrichment could repair the witness.\n- Do not use failure of one construction as a no-go theorem for the full approximation class.\n\n## Mundane-explanation attacks\n\nConstruct the strongest scientifically plausible nuisance explanation, such as:\n\n- effective linearity after matching gain, scale, spectrum, or another nuisance parameter;\n- frozen-feature, lazy, low-rank, or small-motion behavior;\n- interpolation rather than autonomous prediction;\n- observable insensitivity despite wrong internal dynamics;\n- a special symmetry or data degeneracy;\n- an easier regime introduced by the testbed.\n\nUse a matched control that grants the nuisance explanation its best legitimate parameters without giving it the target trajectory. Add a mechanism-specific ablation when possible.\n\nClosing a loophole requires evidence against this strongest version, not against identity dynamics or another straw man.\n\n## Numerical and empirical attacks\n\n- Check conditioning before interpreting high-order behavior.\n- Use a common reference when comparing successive approximations.\n- Separate discretization, quadrature, sampling, optimization, and model errors.\n- Replicate any conclusion that is comparable to numerical variability.\n- Freeze primary metrics and thresholds before inspecting the decisive result.\n- Track negative and failed runs, not only favorable configurations.\n- Distinguish a theory-selected stress test from a searched favorable example.\n- Check source hashes, configurations, seeds, and raw-result provenance.\n- Treat failure of a validity gate as inconclusive, not as evidence for either hypothesis.\n\n## Logical severity\n\nClassify each surviving issue:\n\n- **Fatal:** contradicts the central conjecture under its stated contract.\n- **Witness-fatal:** defeats the current construction but not the existence claim.\n- **Major:** blocks a necessary bridge between claim-ladder rungs.\n- **Conditional:** matters only if a named assumption fails.\n- **Minor:** affects constants, presentation, or a noncentral extension.\n\nDo not call a gap \u201ctechnical\u201d unless a known theorem or fully specified argument appears to close it under already verified hypotheses.\n\n## Evidence language\n\nUse these distinctions:\n\n- **Ruled out:** logically contradicted or rejected by a valid, sufficiently powered discriminator over the stated scope.\n- **Strongly disfavored:** survives logically but conflicts with replicated evidence.\n- **Disfavored:** has contrary evidence with meaningful caveats.\n- **Unsupported:** lacks positive evidence.\n- **Open:** viable evidence exists on both sides or the decisive check is missing.\n- **Inconclusive:** the attempted check failed a logical or numerical validity gate.\n\n\u201cNo failure observed\u201d is not \u201cruled out.\u201d \u201cAccurate on tested observables\u201d is not \u201cmechanism identified.\u201d\n\n## Final hostile questions\n\nBefore upgrading the research claim, answer:\n\n1. What is the strongest surviving ordinary explanation?\n2. What is the strongest structural obstruction?\n3. What numerical artifact could mimic the result?\n4. Which necessary claim-ladder bridge remains assumed?\n5. Would the opposite result have changed the conclusion?\n6. Was that decision rule fixed before observing the result?\n7. Does the evidence generalize beyond the selected instances?\n8. What would still make the central claim false?\n", "truncated": false}}

Do not treat the mode, the user's optimism, or the conjecture's fame as evidence that a proof exists.\n\n## Fix the proof contract\n\nBefore choosing approaches, record:\n\n- the exact theorem, quantifiers, hypotheses, definitions, and edge cases;\n- which classical or supplied results may be used;\n- what counts as a complete proof;\n- which special cases, reductions, computations, or conditional results do not resolve the target;\n- the time, compute, token, and concurrency budget;\n- the terminal outcomes: a proof that survives audit, or budget exhaustion with exact proved results and gaps.\n\nDo not prohibit literature search unless the user requests an independent-discovery or benchmark-integrity mode. Cluster routes that use the same mathematical idea even when their notation differs.\n\n## Enforce a concrete route contract\n\nRequire every route to return:\n\n1. a precise claim or construction;\n2. a derivation or proof of everything asserted;\n3. every external dependency and its checked hypotheses;\n4. a counterexample search or sanity check when applicable;\n5. the exact unresolved implication, if any;\n6. a registry-status recommendation and reason.\n\nReject vague progress reports, unsupported optimism, and claims that a global compatibility step is routine. A reduction is meaningful progress only when its target is already proved, strictly better understood, or accompanied by a new mechanism that attacks it.\n\n## Run adaptive rounds\n\nAfter each round:\n\n1. verify concrete outputs before treating them as progress;\n2. merge duplicate routes by mechanism;\n3. identify reductions whose missing lemma is equivalent in strength to the original theorem;\n4. redirect effort toward underexplored families or newly enabled bottlenecks;\n5. mark a route blocked when it stalls at a theorem-strength lemma;\n6. reopen a blocked route only after a materially new invariant, construction, theorem, or counterexample appears;\n7. cross-pollinate mature routes when one route supplies another's missing ingredient;\n8. preserve valid lemmas and counterexamples from failed routes.\n\nDo not let elegance, early popularity, or the amount of work already invested determine allocation.\n\n## Audit candidate proofs independently\n\nWhen a complete route appears, stop expanding it and begin reconstruction. Assign separate checks, sequentially or through authorized workers, to:\n\n- reconstruct every implication from the proof contract;\n- verify all cited theorem statements and hypotheses;\n- test definitions, degenerate cases, boundary cases, and well-definedness;\n- search for circular dependence or an equivalent-strength hidden lemma;\n- generate the theorem-specific failure checklist;\n- seek a minimal counterexample to every vulnerable intermediate claim;\n- distinguish a complete proof from a plausible proof sketch.\n\nIntegrate the final mathematical argument using the rigorous proof workflow. Label it `candidate proof` until the full chain survives independent audit, and state whether any formal or machine verification was actually performed.\n\n## Stop and report honestly\n\nStop when a complete proof survives the authorized audit, or when the hard budget is exhausted. At exhaustion, report the registry, all proved lemmas, refuted and blocked routes, exact remaining gaps, and each route's reopen condition. Never convert persistence instructions into permission to fabricate a proof or conceal an unresolved implication.\n", "truncated": false}}

The key issue is whether it gives an autonomous approximation—not merely a small error when evaluated along an already known trajectory.

The equations below concern finite feature time; a\nphysical-time conclusion still needs the canonical clock and all\nconvergence requirements in CONTRACT_AND_LEDGER.md.\n\n## 1. Exact integral equations\n\nUse normalized finite inner product and rank-one action\n\\(\\langle u,v\\rangle_n=u^Tv/n\\) and\n\\((u\\otimes v)w=u\\langle v,w\\rangle_n\\). The same formulas make\nsense on the three population Hilbert spaces with their separate\ninner products. It has not disappeared from the nonlinear equation\nthat generates \\(a\\).\n\n## 2. What time regularity proves\n\nOn a fixed feature horizon with the established primal bounds, all\nfour paths in (7) are uniformly Lipschitz in normalized L2. For the\nnew path this is simply\n\\[\n|a(s)-a(t)|_2\\le\\int_t^s|\\delta^{(2)}(u)|_2du\n\\le C_S|s-t|.\n\\tag{8}\n\\]\nThe other three derivative bounds were established in\nPANAHI_EULER_PERTURBATION_SIZE.md. Constants are uniform in width on\nthe stated primal event. These are statements about actual unperturbed\ntrajectories; they are not assumed for a perturbed comparison process.\n\nFor any one such path \\(v\\), a causal left-endpoint mesh of spacing\nat most \\(\\varepsilon/C_S\\) satisfies\n\\[\n\\sup_{s\\le S}|v(s)-v(\\pi(s))|_2\\le\\varepsilon,\n\\qquad\n\\sup_{s\\le S}|W_0v(s)-W_0v(\\pi(s))|_2\n \\le\\|W_0\\|_{\\rm op}\\varepsilon.                          \\tag{9}\n\\]\nConsequently a specified actual path admits a time net of\n\\(1+\\lceil C_SS/\\varepsilon\\rceil\\) matrix arguments per\norientation, independently of width. This is an approximation of\nmatrix responses along a supplied trajectory.\n\nThe rank memories in (1)--(2) do not hide an extra continuity failure\non this uniformly Lipschitz path class. For example, write \\(h=h^{(1)}\\),\nassume both paths have \\(|h|_2\\le B_h\\), \\(|h'|_2\\le L_h\\),\n\\(|a'|_2\\le L_a\\), and let\n\\(e_a=\\sup_s|a(s)-\\widetilde a(s)|_2\\),\n\\(e_h=\\sup_s|h(s)-\\widetilde h(s)|_2\\), with both primitives\nzero initially. Integration by parts gives\n\\[\n\\begin{split}\nM^{(2)}-\\widetilde M^{(2)}\n={}&(a-\\widetilde a)\\otimes h\n -\\int_0^s(a-\\widetilde a)\\otimes h'\\,du\\\\\n &+\\int_0^s\\widetilde a'\\otimes(h-\\widetilde h)\\,du,\n\\end{split}\n\\]\nand hence\n\\[\n\\sup_s\\|M^{(2)}-\\widetilde M^{(2)}\\|_{\\rm op}\n\\le(B_h+SL_h)e_a+SL_ae_h.                                   \\tag{10}\n\\]\nSimilarly, since \\(R=\\int_0^s(M^{(2)})^*da\\),\n\\[\n\\sup_s|R-\\widetilde R|_2\n\\le SL_a\\sup_s\\|M^{(2)}-\\widetilde M^{(2)}\\|_{\n\\rm op}+2SL_aB_h e_a.                                      \\tag{11}\n\\]\nFor (11), integrate\n\\(\\int(\\widetilde M^{(2)})^*d(a-\\widetilde a)\\) by parts,\nusing \\(\\|\\widetilde M^{(2)}\\|\\le SL_aB_h\\) and\n\\(\\|(\\widetilde M^{(2)})'\\|\\le L_aB_h\\). The ordinary\nintegral for \\(M^{(3)}\\) is Lipschitz in the uniform distances of\n\\(\\delta^{(3)},h^{(2)}\\) under their norm bounds.\n\nThus (9) yields small equation residuals after replacing only the\ninitial-matrix responses along the actual trajectory. The exact\nrank-update memory has not been dropped to obtain this statement.\n\n## 3. Why this does not yet construct a finite-query approximation\n\nAt time \\(s_k\\), the value of an actual query such as \\(a(s_k)\\)\nhas been generated by the dynamics on the entire preceding interval.\nThose dynamics used the initial matrices at unrecorded intermediate\narguments. Observing the actual query is causal in physical time,\nbut it is not thereby measurable from only the retained finite-query\ntranscript. Gaussian conditioning on the latter transcript cannot\ntreat that query as a legal next query without proving this\nmeasurability. Sampling a true trajectory does not supply such a proof.\n\nA finite-call algorithm can instead evolve its own state using frozen\nor interpolated matrix responses and make new calls on its own\nqueries. That is an admissible proposed approximation. Equation (9)\non the true path proves consistency of the substitution, not closeness\nbetween this algorithm and the true dynamics. In particular it does\nnot establish the rank premise for the actual perturbed histories in\nPANAHI_ADAPTIVE_GRAM_MARTINGALE.md; query perturbations may generate\nnew directions in subsequent nonlinear queries.\n\nThe uniform temporal bounds also do not by themselves give strong\ncompactness in a population L2 space. Uniformly Lipschitz paths\n\\(s\\mapsto s e_j\\) along orthonormal \\(e_j\\) form an elementary\nbounded noncompact family. This functional-analysis observation is\nnot a claim that such a family occurs in the canonical network.\n\n## 4. The retained stability term and derivative observables\n\nFor two candidate state paths, the exact primitive difference obeys\n\\[\n\\begin{split}\na(s)-\\widetilde a(s)=\\int_0^s\n &\\phi'(z^{(2)})\\odot(q^{(2)}-\\widetilde q^{(2)})\\\\\n &+[\\phi'(z^{(2)})-\\phi'(\\widetilde z^{(2)})]\n       \\odot\\widetilde q^{(2)}\\,du.\n\\end{split}                                                    \\tag{12}\n\\]\nThe first integrand is L2-bounded by \\(|q^{(2)}-\\widetilde\nq^{(2)}|_2\\). In the second integrand, the available L2 norm bound\nfor \\(\\widetilde q^{(2)}\\) does not provide a width-uniform\nLipschitz bound in \\(|z^{(2)}-\\widetilde z^{(2)}|_2\\).\nFor \\(Q>0\\) the exact tail split gives\n\\[\n\\begin{split}\n&|[\\phi'(z)-\\phi'(\\widetilde z)]\\widetilde q|_2\\\\\n&\\quad\\le\\|\\phi''\\|_\\infty Q|z-\\widetilde z|_2\n +2|\\widetilde q\\,\\mathbf1_{\\{|\\widetilde q|>Q\\}}|_2.\n\\end{split}                                                    \\tag{13}\n\\]\nThe total L2 bound alone neither makes the last term uniformly small\nover the approximation family nor supplies the tail-versus-stability\nrate needed to pass \\(Q\\to\\infty\\). Integrating the initial query\ntherefore retains the actual middle multiplier; it does not resolve\nthe continuation problem represented by that multiplier.\n\nThere is also a separate observable issue. The lower backward result\nis\n\\[\n(W^{(2)}(s))^*\\delta^{(2)}(s)\n=\\frac{d}{ds}\\big[(W^{(2)}_0)^*a(s)\\big]\n +(M^{(2)}(s))^*a'(s),                                    \\tag{14}\n\\]\nand \\(\\delta^{(1)}=\\phi'(z^{(1)})\\odot\n(W^{(2)})^*\\delta^{(2)}\\). Uniform approximation of the integrated\nresponse in (9) does not imply strong convergence of its derivative.\nFor example, \\(j^{-1}\\sin(js)v\\) tends uniformly to zero with\nuniformly bounded derivative, while its derivatives do not tend to\nzero in time L2 for a nonzero fixed \\(v\\). This is a limitation of\nthe inference, not a canonical counterexample. Hidden velocities and\nthe raw first kernel block require additional derivative or energy\ncontrol even if uniform state convergence were separately proved.\n\n## Conclusion\n\nThe exact initial-matrix state queries can all be made time-Lipschitz\nby passing to the lower backward primitive. Their supplied-trajectory\ncovering number is width-independent, and the full rank memories have\nthe continuity bounds (10)--(11). This improves the representation.\nIt does not establish a finite-transcript causal approximation,\nnonlinear removal, strong compactness, unique autonomous restart, or\nthe required velocity/kernel convergence. The decisive retained term\nis (12), with the distinct derivative obligation (14).\n", "truncated": false}}
